\displaystyle g_{j}(t)\leq\displaystyle\frac{t^{K-j}}{(K-j)!}\cdot g_{K}(t)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=j+1}^{K}h_{i}(t).

Proof.

We verify (
4.4
) by induction on
K-j
. Since (
4.4
) holds at
K-j=0
, let us fix
j_{0}\in\llbracket 0,K-1\rrbracket
and prove that (
4.4
) holds for
K-j=K-j_{0}
, assuming it holds whenever
K-j\leq K-j_{0}-1
. This follows from the estimates

\displaystyle g_{j_{0}}(t)

\displaystyle\leq h_{j_{0}+1}(t)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}g_{j_{0}+1}(s)ds

\displaystyle\leq h_{j_{0}+1}(t)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\frac{s^{K-j_{0}-1}}{(K-j_{0}-1)!}\cdot g_{K}(s)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=j_{0}+2}^{K}h_{i}(s)ds

\displaystyle\leq g_{K}(t)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=j_{0}+1}^{K}h_{i}(t)\cdot\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\frac{s^{K-j_{0}-1}}{(K-j_{0}-1)!}ds

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{t^{K-j_{0}}}{(K-j_{0})!}\cdot g_{K}(t)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=j_{0}+1}^{K}h_{i}(t),

where the first bound holds by the
k=j_{0}
case of (
4.3
); the second by the inductive hypothesis; the third by the fact that the
g_{i}
and
h_{i}
are nondecreasing; and the fourth by performing the integration.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
.

Observe from (
2.4
) that, for any
(i,s)\in\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(a_{i}-\tilde{a}_{i})=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot(b_{i}-b_{i+1})(a_{i}-\tilde{a}_{i})+\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{a}_{i}}{2}\cdot\big((b_{i}-\tilde{b}_{i})-(b_{i+1}-\tilde{b}_{i+1})\big);

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(b_{i}-\tilde{b}_{i})=(a_{i-1}-\tilde{a}_{i-1})(a_{i-1}+\tilde{a}_{i-1})-(a_{i}-\tilde{a}_{i})(a_{i}+\tilde{a}_{i}),

where we have abbreviated
(\tilde{a}_{i},a_{i};\tilde{b}_{i},b_{i})=(\tilde{a}_{i}(s),a_{i}(s);\tilde{b}_{i}(s),b_{i}(s))
. Using (
4.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}a_{i}(s)-\partial_{t}\tilde{a}_{i}(s)|

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot|b_{i}-b_{i+1}|\cdot G_{i}(s)+|\tilde{a}_{i}|\cdot G_{\llbracket i,i+1\rrbracket}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot H_{\llbracket i,i+1\rrbracket}(s)\cdot G_{\llbracket i,i+1\rrbracket}(s),

and

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}b_{i}(s)-\partial_{t}\tilde{b}_{i}(s)|

\displaystyle=|a_{i-1}+\tilde{a}_{i-1}|\cdot G_{i-1}(s)+|a_{i}+\tilde{a}_{i}|\cdot G_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\cdot H_{\llbracket i-1,i\rrbracket}(s)\cdot G_{\llbracket i-1,i\rrbracket}(s).

Summing these two bounds, fixing
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, integrating over
s\in[0,t_{0}]
, and using (
4.1
) yields

\displaystyle\big|a_{i}(t_{0})-\tilde{a}_{i}(t_{0})\big|+\big|b_{i}(t_{0})-\tilde{b}_{i}(t_{0})\big|\leq H_{\llbracket i-1,i+1\rrbracket}(t_{0})\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{0}}G_{\llbracket i-1,i+1\rrbracket}(s)ds,

for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, where we have also used that
a_{i}(0)=\tilde{a}_{i}(0)
and
b_{i}(0)=\tilde{b}_{i}(0)
for such
i
. Taking the supremum over
t_{0}\in[0,t]
, we deduce that (
4.3
) holds for any
(k,t)\in\llbracket 0,K-1\rrbracket\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, if we set

\displaystyle g_{k}(t)=G_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+K-k,N_{2}^{\prime}-K+k\rrbracket}(t);\qquad h_{k}(t)=H_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+K-k,N_{2}^{\prime}-K+k\rrbracket}(t).

The lemma then follows from the
(j,t)=(0,T)
case of
Lemma

4.2
.
∎

The next proposition compares a Toda lattice
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
on a torus to one
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(t),\tilde{\bm{b}}(t))
on an interval (of the same size), if the two coincide on a subinterval of their domains. Its proof is entirely analogous to that of
Proposition

4.1
and is therefore omitted.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
N_{1}\leq N_{2}
be integers; set
N=N_{2}-N_{1}+1
. For each real number
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, fix
N
-tuples
\tilde{\bm{a}}(t),\bm{a}(t),\tilde{\bm{b}}(t),\bm{b}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, indexed as

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{a}}(t)=(\tilde{a}_{N_{1}}(t),\tilde{a}_{N_{1}+1}(t),\ldots,\tilde{a}_{N_{2}}(t));\qquad\tilde{\bm{b}}(t)=(\tilde{b}_{N_{1}}(t),\tilde{b}_{N_{1}+1}(t),\ldots,\tilde{b}_{N_{2}}(t));

\displaystyle\bm{a}(t)=(a_{0}(t),a_{1}(t),\ldots,a_{N-1}(t));\qquad\quad\bm{b}(t)=(b_{0}(t),b_{1}(t),\ldots,b_{N-1}(t)).

For each
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, also set
\tilde{a}_{i}(s)=0=\tilde{b}_{i}(s)
if
i\notin\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}-1\rrbracket
; additionally set
a_{i}(s)=a_{i+N}(s)
and
b_{i}(s)=b_{i+N}(s)
for each
(i,s)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Assume that
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(t);\tilde{\bm{b}}(t))
satisfies (
2.4
) for each
(j,t)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and that
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
satisfies (
2.4
) for each
(j,t)\in\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Under this notation, for any
I\leq J
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, define
G_{\llbracket I,J\rrbracket}(t)
and
H_{\llbracket I,J\rrbracket}(t)
as in (
4.1
).

Now let
K\geq 1
and
N_{1}^{\prime}\leq N_{2}^{\prime}
be integers such that
N_{1}\leq N_{1}^{\prime}\leq N_{2}^{\prime}\leq N_{2}
and
N_{1}^{\prime}+K\leq N_{2}^{\prime}-K
. If
a_{j}(0)=\tilde{a}_{j}(0)
and
b_{j}(0)=\tilde{b}_{j}(0)
for each
j\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, then (
4.2
) holds for any
T\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

4.2.
Approximate Thermal Equilibrium for the Open Toda Lattice

Unlike for the periodic Toda lattice, thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N-1,N}
(from
Definition

2.4
) is not an invariant measure for the open Toda lattice. In this section we establish the below proposition indicating that it, in a certain sense, still “approximately is,” if time scale is much shorter than the domain size. Its proof uses
Proposition

4.3
to couple the periodic Toda lattice to the closed one.

Proposition 4.4
.

Adopt
Assumption

2.8
, and fix
t\in[0,T]
. There exists a random matrix
\bm{M}=[M_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
, whose law coincides with that of
\bm{L}(0)
, such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any real number
K\geq T\log N
, we have that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|L_{ij}(t)-M_{ij}|\leq e^{-K/5}.

To establish
Proposition

4.4
, we first require the following quick consequence of
Proposition

4.1
and
Proposition

4.3
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of either
Proposition

4.1
or
Proposition

4.3
, and suppose that
T\geq 1
. Fix a real number
A\geq 1
, and assume that
K\geq 200AT
and that
|a_{i}(0)|+|\tilde{a}_{i}(0)|+|b_{i}(0)|+|\tilde{b}_{i}(0)|\leq A
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,
G_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+K,N_{2}^{\prime}-K\rrbracket}(T)\leq e^{-K/4}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.5
, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle G_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(T)\leq H_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(T)

\displaystyle\leq 36\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(|a_{i}(0)|+|b_{i}(0)|+|\tilde{a}_{i}(0)|+|\tilde{b}_{i}(0)|\big)

(4.7)

\displaystyle\leq 36A.

Thus,

\displaystyle G_{\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+K,N_{2}^{\prime}-K\rrbracket}(T)\leq\displaystyle\frac{T^{K}}{K!}(36A)^{K+1}

\displaystyle\leq 36A\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{36eAT}{K}\bigg)^{K}

\displaystyle\leq 36A\cdot 2^{-K}\leq 2^{-K/2}\leq e^{-K/4}.

where in the first inequality we used
Proposition

4.1
with (
4.6
); in the second we used the fact that
K!\geq(e^{-1}K)^{K}
; in the third we used the fact that
36eATK^{-1}\leq 100ATK^{-1}\leq 1/2
; in the fourth we used the fact that
2^{-K/2}\leq 2^{-100AT}\leq(36A)^{-1}
(as
A,T\geq 1
); and in the fifth we used the fact that
e\leq 4
. This establishes the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.4
.

We will compare the open Lax matrix
\bm{L}(t)
to a periodic one
\bm{R}(t)
. To prescribe initial data for the latter (through its Flaschka variables), define
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(0),\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(0))
, where
\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(0)=(\mathfrak{a}_{j}(0))
and
\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(0)=(\mathfrak{b}_{j}(0))
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, as follows. First, for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
, set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{i}(0)=L_{i,i+1}(0)=a_{i}(0),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{i}(0)=L_{i,i}(0)=b_{i}(0).

Letting
\mathfrak{r}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
denote a Gamma random variable, independent from
\bm{L}(0)
, with density
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{r}\in(r,r+dr)]=2\beta^{\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}\cdot r^{2\theta-1}e^{-\beta r^{2}}dr
, further set
\mathfrak{a}_{N_{2}}=\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{N_{2}}=L_{N_{2},N_{2}}(0)=b_{N_{2}}(0)
. We then extend these Flaschka variables periodically, by imposing
(\mathfrak{a}_{j+N}(0);\mathfrak{b}_{j+N}(0))=(\mathfrak{a}_{j}(0);\mathfrak{b}_{j}(0))
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this way,
(\mathfrak{a}_{j}(0);\mathfrak{b}_{j}(0))
over
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
is sampled under
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N}
from
Definition

3.3
.

Next, let
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(t);\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(t))
, where
\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(t)=(\mathfrak{a}_{j}(t))
and
\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(t)=(\mathfrak{b}_{j}(t))
, denote the solution to the periodic Toda lattice (
2.4
) on the torus
\mathbb{T}_{N}
, with initial data
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(0);\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(0))
. Here,
(j,t)
ranges over
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, imposing the periodicity constraint
(\mathfrak{a}_{j+N}(t);\mathfrak{b}_{j+N}(t))=(\mathfrak{a}_{j}(t);\mathfrak{b}_{j}(t))
. It follows from
Lemma

3.4
that, for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\text{the law of}\quad(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(t);\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(t))\quad\text{coincides with that of}\quad(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(0);\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(0)).

Define the associated Lax matrix
10
10

10

This convention is slightly different from the one in
Definition

3.1
(where the rows and columns are indexed by
\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket
instead of
\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
); we use it for notational convenience.

\bm{R}(t)=[R_{ij}(t)]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
, by setting
R_{jj}(t)=\mathfrak{b}_{j}(t)
and
R_{j,j+1}(t)=R_{j+1,j}(t)=\mathfrak{a}_{j}(t)
for each
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, where we have denoted
R_{N_{2}+1,N_{2}}(t)=R_{N_{1},N_{2}}(t)
and
R_{N_{2},N_{2}+1}(t)=R_{N_{2},N_{1}}(t)
. If
(i,j)
is not of the above form, then we set
R_{ij}(t)=0
.

Now define
\bm{M}=[M_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
by setting the
(N_{1},N_{2})
-entry and
(N_{2},N_{1})
-entry of
\bm{R}(T)
to
0
, namely
M_{ij}=\mathbbm{1}_{(i,j)\neq(N_{1},N_{2})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(i,j)\neq(N_{2},N_{1})}\cdot R_{ij}(T)
. By (
4.8
), the fact that
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(0);\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(0))
is sampled under
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N}
; and
Assumption

2.8
,
\bm{M}
has the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
.

It therefore remains to confirm that (
4.5
) holds with high probability. To that end, recalling
Definition

3.13
, define the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{R}(s)}(\log N/800)
. By
Lemma

3.15
,
\mathsf{E}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable, so we will restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows.

Now we apply
Proposition

4.3
and
Lemma

4.5
. More specifically, since
L_{ij}(0)=R_{ij}(0)
for
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+1,N_{2}-1\rrbracket
, applying
Lemma

4.5
(with the
(A;I,J;K)
there equal to
(\log N/200;N_{1}+1,N_{2}-1;K-1)
here, using the fact that
(K-1)/4\leq K/5
for sufficiently large
N
, and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
), yields (
4.5
), since
M_{ij}=R_{ij}(t)
for
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
. ∎

4.3.
Infinite Volume Limit

Recall that our previous descriptions of the Toda lattice involved a finite number of variables, and were thus defined on a finite domain. In this section we explain conditions under which solutions of the Toda lattice on infinite domains can be realized as limits of those on finite domains. These conditions are described through the below assumption.

Assumption 4.6
.

Fix real numbers
R>1>\mathfrak{p}\geq 0
. For each real number
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and integers
n\geq 1\geq m
, let

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{[m,n]}(t)=(a_{j}^{[m,n]}(t))_{j\in\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n+1};

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{(n)}(t)=(a_{j}^{(n)}(t))_{j\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1};\quad\bm{a}=(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}};

\displaystyle\bm{b}^{[m,n]}(t)=(b_{j}^{[m,n]}(t))_{j\in\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n+1};

\displaystyle\bm{b}^{(n)}(t)=(b_{j}^{(n)}(t))_{j\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1};\quad\bm{b}=(b_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},

be
(m+n+1)
-tuples,
(2n+1)
-tuples, and infinite sequences of real numbers. Assume that
(\bm{a}^{[m,n]}(t);\bm{b}^{[m,n]}(t))
is a solution to the open Toda lattice (
2.4
) on the interval
\llbracket m,n\rrbracket
, and that
(\bm{a}^{(n)}(t);\bm{b}^{(n)}(t))
is a solution to the periodic Toda lattice (
2.4
) on the torus
\mathbb{T}_{2n+1}
, which we identify with
\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket
. Further assume for any integers
n\geq 1\geq m
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|a_{j}^{[m,n]}(0)|+|a_{j}^{[m,n]}(0)|\leq R(|j|+1)^{\mathfrak{p}};\\
&|a_{j}^{(n)}(0)|+|b_{j}^{(n)}(0)|\leq R(|j|+1)^{\mathfrak{p}}.\end{aligned}

Also assume
(a_{j}^{(n)}(0),b_{j}^{(n)}(0))=(a_{j},b_{j})
whenever
j\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket
; that
b_{j}^{[m,n]}(0)=b_{j}
whenever
j\in\llbracket m,n\rrbracket
; and that
a_{j}^{[m,n]}(0)=a_{j}
whenever
j\in\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket
(and
a_{n}^{[m,n]}(0)=0
).

Proposition 4.7
.

Adopt
Assumption

4.6
. Then, for each
(j,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, there exist real numbers
a_{j}(t)
and
b_{j}(t)
such that

(4.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t)=a_{j}(t)=\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{j}^{(n)}(t);\qquad\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}b_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t)=b_{j}(t)=\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}b_{j}^{(n)}(t).

Moreover, denoting
\bm{a}(t)=(a_{j}(t))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\bm{b}(t)=(b_{j}(t))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we have that
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
solves the Toda lattice (
2.4
) for each
(j,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Observe by (
4.9
) and
Lemma

3.5
that, for any integers
n\geq 1\geq m
and real number
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{j\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket}\big(|a_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t)|+|b_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t)|+|a_{j}^{(n)}(t)|+|b_{j}^{(n)}(t)|\big)\leq 12R(n+1)^{\mathfrak{p}}.

Now let
K\geq 1
be an integer, and let
N\geq 1
be an integer. By
Proposition

4.3
, with the last statement in
Assumption

4.6
(and the fact that
72R(n+1)^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq 144Rn^{\mathfrak{p}}
for
n\geq 1
), we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle\big|a_{j}^{(N)}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{(N)}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{t^{K}}{K!}\cdot(144RN^{\mathfrak{p}})^{K+1},

for any
(j,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
with
|j|+K\leq N
. Thus, applying (
4.12
) at
(N,K)=\big(n,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)
, we find

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big(\big|a_{j}^{(n)}(t)-a_{j}^{[-n,n]}(

\displaystyle t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{(n)}(t)-b_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t)\big|\big)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\displaystyle\frac{t^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor!}\cdot(144Rn^{\mathfrak{p}})^{n/2+1}=0,

where in the last equality we used the fact that
\mathfrak{p}<1
. Hence, it suffices to show that the first limits in both of the pairs of equalities in (
4.10
) hold.

To that end, fix an integer
J\geq 1
and a real number
T\geq 0
. For
(j,t)\in\llbracket-J,J\rrbracket\times[0,T]
, we first bound
(a_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t),b_{j}^{[-n,n]}(t))
independently of
n
. To do this, observe (as used to obtain (
4.12
)) by
Proposition

4.1
with the last statement in
Assumption

4.6
that, for any integers
N\geq N^{\prime}\geq 1
,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\big|a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N^{\prime},N^{\prime}]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{[N,N]}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N^{\prime},N^{\prime}]}(t)\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{T^{K}}{K!}\cdot(144RN^{\mathfrak{p}})^{K+1},

for any
(j,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
with
|j|+K\leq N^{\prime}
. Now let
N_{0}>N_{1}>\cdots>N_{r}
be integers such that
N_{0}=N
, such that
N_{i}/16\leq N_{i+1}\leq N_{i}/4
for each
i\in\llbracket 0,r-1\rrbracket
, and such that
N_{r}\in[4J,16J]
. Applying (
4.13
) with the
(N,N^{\prime};K)
there equal to
(N_{i},N_{i+1};N_{i+1}/2)
here, we find that there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\mathfrak{p},R,T)>1
such that

\displaystyle\big|a_{j}^{[-N_{i},N_{i}]}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N_{i+1},N_{i+1}]}(t)\big|+\big|

\displaystyle b_{j}^{[-N_{i},N_{i}]}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N_{i+1},N_{i+1}]}(t)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{T^{K}}{K!}\cdot(144RN_{i}^{\mathfrak{p}})^{K+1}\leq C_{1}e^{-N_{i}},

where in the last bound we used the facts that
K=N_{i+1}/2\geq N_{i}/32
and
\mathfrak{p}<1
. Hence, for any integer
m\in\llbracket 1,r\rrbracket
, we have

(4.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|&a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N_{m},N_{m}]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N_{m},N_{m}]}(t)\big|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\Big(\big|a_{j}^{[-N_{i},N_{i}]}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N_{i+1},N_{i+1}]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{[-N_{i},N_{i}]}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N_{i+1},N_{i+1}]}(t)\big|\Big)\\
&\leq C_{1}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}e^{-N_{i}}\leq 2C_{1}e^{-N_{m}}.\end{aligned}

Taking
m=r
and using (
4.11
) (with the
n
there equal to
N_{r}\in[4J,16J]
here), it follows that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket-J,J\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(|a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)|+|b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)|\big)&\leq 2C_{1}e^{-N_{r}}+12R(N_{r}+1)^{\mathfrak{p}}\\
&\leq 250RJ+2C_{1}.\end{aligned}

By (
4.14
) and (
4.13
),
(a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t))
and
(b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t))
are Cauchy sequences over
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Hence, these sequences admit unique limits
a_{j}(t)
and
b_{j}(t)
, respectively, as
N
tends to
\infty
. This establishes the first part of the proposition.

To establish the second, observe by (
4.15
) that there exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\mathfrak{p},R,J,T)>1
such that
|a_{j}(t)|+|b_{j}(t)|\leq C_{2}
and
|a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)|+|b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)|\leq C_{2}
, for all
(j,t)\in\llbracket-J,J\rrbracket\times[0,T]
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Since
(\bm{a}^{[-N,N]}(t);\bm{b}^{[-N,N]}(t))
satisfies (
2.4
), these estimates imply that the first two
t
-derivatives of
a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)
and
b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)
are uniformly bounded, for all
(j,t)\in\llbracket-J,J\rrbracket\times[0,T]
. Thus, the
t
-derivatives of
a_{j}(t)
and
b_{j}(t)
are also uniformly bounded for all
(j,t)\in\llbracket-J,J\rrbracket\times[0,T]
. Together with the fact that
(\bm{a}^{[-N,N]}(t);\bm{b}^{[-N,N]}(t))
satisfy (
2.4
), this implies that
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
satisfy (
2.4
), thereby confirming the second statement of the proposition.
∎

The below corollary indicates that the Toda lattice on the full line
\mathbb{Z}
at thermal equilibrium can be defined by taking a limit of periodic Toda lattices at thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N}
(recall
Definition

3.3
). Here, for any integer
N\geq 1
, we identify the torus
\mathbb{T}_{2N+1}
with the interval
\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
(instead of
\llbracket 0,2N\rrbracket
), which will index the Flaschka variables of the associated Toda lattice.

Corollary 4.8
.

Fix
\beta,\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
be mutually independent random variables, so that
(a_{j},b_{j})
has law
\mu_{\beta,\theta;1}
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. For each integer
N\geq 1
, let
(\bm{a}^{(N)}(t),\bm{b}^{(N)}(t))
denote the periodic Toda lattice (
2.4
) on
\mathbb{T}_{2N+1}
, with initial data
(\bm{a}^{(N)}(0);\bm{b}^{(N)}(0))
given by
\bm{a}^{(N)}(0)=(a_{-N},a_{1-N},\ldots,a_{N})
and
\bm{b}^{(N)}(0)=(b_{-N},b_{1-N},\ldots,b_{N})
.

(1)

For each
(j,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the limits
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}a_{j}^{(N)}(t)=a_{j}(t)
and
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}b_{j}^{(N)}(t)=b_{j}(t)
exist almost surely; are finite; and solve (
2.4
).

(2)

Denoting
\bm{a}(t)=(a_{j}(t))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\bm{b}(t)=(b_{j}(t))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
, the law of
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
coincides with that of
(\bm{a}(0);\bm{b}(0))
, for any
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

The second statement of the corollary follows from the first, together with
Lemma

3.4
. It therefore remains to show the first, to which end it suffices by
Proposition

4.7
to verify
Assumption

4.6
, that is, to show that there almost surely exists a constant
R>1
such that
|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|\leq R(|j|+1)^{1/2}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. To that end, observe that there exists a constant
C>1
such that, for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}\big[|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|>2(|j|+1)^{1/2}\big]&\leq\mathbb{P}\big[a_{j}>(|j|+1)^{1/2}\big]+\mathbb{P}\big[|b_{j}|>(|j|+1)^{1/2}\big]\\
&\leq C|j|^{\theta+1}e^{-\beta|j|/2},\end{aligned}

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
(a_{j},b_{j})
has law
\mu_{\beta,\theta;1}
given by
Definition

3.3
. Since the sum of the right side of (
4.16
) over
j\in\mathbb{Z}
is finite, the Borel–Cantelli lemma implies the almost sure existence of a constant
R>1
such that
|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|\leq R(|j|+1)^{1/2}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. ∎

We can now establish
Corollary

4.8
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.5
.

This will follow from using
Lemma

4.5
to compare the open Toda lattice
(\bm{a}^{[-N,-N]}(t),\bm{b}^{[-N,-N]}(t))
to one on the torus
\mathbb{T}_{2N+1}
with the same initial data, and then applying
Corollary

4.8
(and its proof) to confirm the large
N
limit of the latter. So let
(\breve{\bm{a}}(t),\breve{\bm{b}}(t))
, where
\breve{\bm{a}}(t)=(\breve{a}_{-N}(t),\breve{a}_{1-N}(t),\ldots,\breve{a}_{N}(t))
and
\breve{\bm{b}}(t)=(\breve{b}_{-N}(t),\breve{b}_{1-N}(t),\ldots,\breve{b}_{N}(t))
, denote the periodic Toda lattice (
2.4
) on
\mathbb{T}_{2N+1}
, with initial data obtained by setting
\breve{a}_{j}(0)=a_{j}
and
\breve{b}_{j}(0)=b_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
. Denoting for each integer
k\geq 2
the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{k}=\bigcap_{j=-k}^{k}\Big\{|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{1600}\cdot\big(R+\log(|k|+1)\big)\Big\};\qquad\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{k=N}^{\infty}\mathsf{E}_{k},

we have from the explicit densities of
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
that
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{k}^{\complement}]\leq c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(R+\log k)^{2}}
, for some constant
c_{1}>0
. Hence, a union bound yields
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}R^{2}}
for some constant
c_{2}>0
(as
R\geq\log N
).

Restricting to
\mathsf{E}
, we then apply
Lemma

4.5
, with the
(N_{1},N_{2},A,K)
;
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
; and
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(t);\tilde{\bm{b}}(t))
there equal to
(-N,N,R/200,K)
;
(\breve{\bm{a}}(t);\breve{\bm{b}}(t))
; and
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
here, respectively. This yields

(4.17)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket K-N,N-K\rrbracket}\big(\big|a_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)-\breve{a}_{j}(t)\big|+|b_{j}^{[-N,N]}(t)-\breve{b}_{j}(t)\big|\big)\leq e^{-K/4}.

Letting
N
(and then
K
) tend to
\infty
, the first two statements of the proposition therefore follow from
Corollary

4.8
(with the
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
there equal to
(\breve{\bm{a}}(t);\breve{\bm{b}}(t))
here).

To confirm the third, we assume that
N_{2}\geq-N_{1}
, as the case when
-N_{1}>N_{2}
is entirely analogous. We will first use (
4.14
) to estimate the difference between
(\bm{a}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t);\bm{b}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t))
and
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
, and then use
Lemma

4.5
to estimate the difference between
(\bm{a}^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t);\bm{b}^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t))
and
(\bm{a}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t);\bm{b}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t))
. To implement the former, first observe that the assumption (
4.9
) holds in
Proposition

4.7
(with the
R
there uniformly bounded for fixed
\mathfrak{p}\in(0,1)
), since on
\mathsf{E}
we have
|a_{j}|\leq(R+\log j)/800\leq(j^{\mathfrak{p}}+\log j)/800\leq(|j|+1)^{\mathfrak{p}}
for sufficiently large
j
. Therefore, (
4.14
) applies, with the
(N,N_{m})
there equal to
(\mathfrak{N},N_{2})
here for some
\mathfrak{N}\geq 4^{m}N_{2}\geq 4^{m-1}N
. This yields for some constant
11
11

11

By our assumption
RT\leq K\leq N^{\mathfrak{p}}
, it is quickly verified using (
4.13
) that this constant is independent of
T
.

C_{1}=C_{1}(\mathfrak{p})>0
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(\big|a_{j}^{[-\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{N}]}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}^{[-\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{N}]}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|\big)\leq C_{1}e^{-N_{2}}.

Letting
m
, and thus
\mathfrak{N}
, tend to
\infty
(and using the fact that
N_{2}\geq N/3
, as
N_{2}\geq-N_{1}
), we deduce

(4.18)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(\big|a_{j}(t)-a_{j}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|+\big|b_{j}(t)-b_{j}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|\big)\leq C_{1}e^{-N/3}.

We next apply
Lemma

4.5
, with the
(N_{1},N_{2};\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2};A,K)
;
(\bm{a}(t);\bm{b}(t))
; and
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(t);\tilde{\bm{b}}(t))
there equal to
(N_{1},N_{2};-N_{2},N_{2};R/200,K)
;
(\bm{a}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t);\bm{b}^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t))
; and
(\bm{a}^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t);\bm{b}^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t))
here, respectively (using our restriction to
\mathsf{E}
, and the fact that
R\geq\log N
, to verify its hypotheses). As in the derivation of (
4.17
), this yields

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}+K\rrbracket}\big(\big|a^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t)-a^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big|a^{[N_{1},N_{2}]}(t)-a^{[-N_{2},N_{2}]}(t)\big|\big)\leq e^{-K/4}.

Together with (
4.18
) (and the fact that
K\leq N
), this yields the third part of the corollary.
∎

5.
Localization Centers

Throughout this section, we fix
\beta,\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; the constants below may depend on them, even if not explicitly stated. We further let
N_{1}\leq N_{2}
be integers and set
N=N_{2}-N_{1}+1
.

5.1.
Existence and Speed Bounds for Localization Centers

In this section we discuss localization centers (recall
Definition

2.6
) and their properties. We begin by confirming their existence, by proving
Lemma

2.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.7
.

Let
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{N})
denote an orthonormal eigenbasis for
\bm{M}
; assume that the rows and columns of
\bm{M}
are indexed by
\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. For each index
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, denote
\bm{u}_{j}=(u_{j}(N_{1}),u_{j}(N_{1}+1),\ldots,u_{j}(N_{2}))
and define the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{j}=\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:|u_{j}(i)|\geq(2N)^{-1}\}.

We must show that there exists a bijection
\varphi:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
such that
\varphi(j)\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. To do this, it suffices by Hall’s theorem to show for any set
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
that
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}|\geq|\mathcal{J}|
, where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}=\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{I}_{j}
. If this statement were false for some subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, then we would have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{J}|-1\geq|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}|=\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}|u_{j}(i)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}|u_{j}(i)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|u_{j}(i)|^{2}-|\mathcal{J}|N(2N)^{-2}

\displaystyle\geq|\mathcal{J}|-\displaystyle\frac{1}{4},

where the first bound follows from the assumption; the second from the orthonormality of the eigenbasis
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{N})
; the third from restricting to
j\in\mathcal{J}
; the fourth from using that
|u_{j}(i)|<(2N)^{-1}
for
i\notin\mathcal{I}_{j}
; and the fifth from the fact that each
\bm{u}_{j}
is a unit vector. This is a contradiction, so a
(2N)^{-1}
-localization center bijection for
\bm{M}
exists.
∎

The next lemma concerns a random Lax matrix corresponding to the open Toda lattice, sampled under thermal equilibrium (recall
Section

3.5
). It first states that its eigenvectors are exponentially localized around their localization centers; it then states (as a quick consequence) that such localization centers are essentially unique, up to an error of
(\log N)^{2}/2
.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Adopt
Assumption

2.8
, but assume more generally that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. Fix any index
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and let
\varphi_{j}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote any
\zeta
-localization center for
\bm{u}_{j}
.

(1)

For any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|i-\varphi_{j}|\geq(\log N)^{2}/2
, we have
|u_{j}(i)|\leq e^{-c|i-\varphi_{j}|}
.

(2)

If
i\in\llbracket N_{2},N_{2}\rrbracket
satisfies
|i-\varphi_{j}|\geq(\log N)^{2}/2
, then
i
is not a
\zeta
-localization center for
\bm{u}_{j}
.

Proof.

The first statement of the lemma implies the second (as
c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}/2}<\zeta
for sufficiently large
N
), so it suffices to verify the former. To that end, observe for some
c>0
that

\displaystyle\zeta^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[|u_{j}(i)|]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[|u_{j}(i)\cdot u_{j}(\varphi_{j})|\big]\leq c^{-1}Ne^{-c|i-\varphi_{j}|}.

Here, the first inequality holds by the definition of
\varphi_{j}
; the second by summing
Corollary

3.17
over
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
(and examining its
(k,j)
term equal to
(j,\varphi_{j})
here). This, with a Markov estimate, a union bound, and the facts that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
and
|i-\varphi_{j}|\geq(\log N)^{2}/2
, yields the first statement of the lemma.
∎

The next lemma bounds the “speed” at which localization centers can move, for a Lax matrix initially at thermal equilibrium.

Lemma 5.2
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Adopt
Assumption

2.8
, and assume more generally that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. For each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, let
\varphi_{j}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote any
\zeta
-localization center for
\bm{u}_{j}(0)
. Then, for each
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\times[0,T]
, we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle|u_{j}(m;s)|\leq e^{-c|m-\varphi_{j}|},\qquad\text{for any $m\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket$ with $|m-\varphi_{j}|\geq T(\log N)^{2}$}.

Moreover,
m
is not a localization center for
\bm{u}_{j}(s)
whenever
|m-\varphi_{j}|\geq T(\log N)^{2}
.

Proof.

The first statement (
5.1
) of the lemma implies the second, as
e^{-c|m-\varphi_{j}|}<\zeta
for
|m-\varphi_{j}|\geq T(\log N)^{2}
if
N
is sufficiently large; it therefore suffices to establish (
5.1
). Recalling
Definition

3.13
and letting
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,1)
denote the constant
c
from
Lemma

5.1
, and define the event

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(s)}(\log N)\cap\bigcap_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\big\{\mathbbm{1}_{|i-\varphi_{j}|\geq(\log N)^{2}/2}\cdot|u_{j}(i;0)|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}|i-\varphi_{j}|}\big\}.

By
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

5.1
,
\mathsf{E}
is overwhelmingly probable, so we restrict to
\mathsf{E}
for the remainder of this proof.

We next recall a fact concerning the evolution of the Lax matrix
\bm{L}(s)
. For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define the tridiagonal skew-symmetric matrix
\bm{P}(s)=[P_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
as follows. For each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
, set
P_{i,i+1}(s)=a_{i}(s)/2
and
P_{i+1,i}(s)=-a_{i}(s)/2
; for all
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket^{2}
not of the above form, set
P_{i,j}(s)=0
. For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
, further let
\bm{V}(s)=[V_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
, satisfying the ordinary differential equation
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(s)
, with initial data
\bm{V}(0)=\Id
; the existence of such a matrix
\bm{V}(s)
follows from the Picard–Lindelöf theorem. For any
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket^{2}
, its
(i,j)
-entry is more explicitly given by

(5.3)

\displaystyle V_{ij}(s)=\mathbbm{1}_{i=j}+\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\cdots\displaystyle\sum_{i_{k-1}=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\cdots\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s_{1}>s_{2}>\cdots>s_{k}}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{k}P_{i_{h-1},i_{h}}(s_{h})ds_{h},

where we have denoted
(i_{0},i_{k})=(i,j)
. Then, by
[
47
, Section 2]
, we have
\bm{V}(s)^{-1}\cdot\bm{L}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(s)=\bm{L}(0)
.

This implies that
\bm{L}(s)=\bm{V}(s)\cdot\bm{L}(0)\cdot\bm{V}(s)^{\mathsf{T}}
, as
\bm{V}(s)
is orthogonal (since
\bm{V}(0)=\Id
,
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(s)
, and
\bm{P}(s)
is skew-symmetric). Hence, letting
\bm{U}(s)=[U_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{N\times N}
denote matrix of eigenvectors of
\bm{L}(s)
, whose
(i,j)
-entry is given by
U_{ij}(s)=u_{j}(i;s)
for each
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, we have
\bm{U}(s)=\bm{V}(s)\cdot\bm{U}(0)
. In particular,

(5.4)

\displaystyle u_{j}(i;s)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}V_{ik}(s)\cdot u_{j}(k;0).

Now observe whenever
|i-j|\geq 20T\log N
that

(5.5)

\displaystyle|V_{ij}(s)|\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{s^{k}}{k!}\cdot(2\log N)^{k}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{2es\log N}{k}\bigg)^{k}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}e^{-k}\leq 2e^{-|i-j|}.

Here, in the first inequality we used (
5.3
), with the facts that each
|P_{ij}(s_{h})|\leq\log N
(as we have restricted to the event
\mathsf{E}
from (
5.2
)) and that
P_{ij}=0
whenever
|i-j|\neq 1
(meaning that there are at most two choices for each
i_{h}
that gives rise to a nonzero summand in (
5.3
)); in the second we used the bound
k!\geq(e^{-1}k)^{k}
for each
k\geq 0
; in the third we used the bound
2k^{-1}es\log N\leq e^{-1}
for
k\geq|i-j|\geq 20T\log N
; and in the fourth we performed the sum. Hence, for
|m-\varphi_{j}|>T(\log N)^{2}
,

\displaystyle|u_{j}(m;s)|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-m|\geq|m-\varphi_{j}|/2}\cdot|V_{mk}(s)|+\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-m|\leq|m-\varphi_{j}|/2}\cdot|u_{j}(k;0)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-m|\geq|m-\varphi_{j}|/2}\cdot e^{-|k-m|}+\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-\varphi_{j}|\geq|m-\varphi_{j}|/2}\cdot\mathfrak{c}^{-1}e^{-\mathfrak{c}|k-\varphi_{j}|}

\displaystyle\leq 16\mathfrak{c}^{-2}e^{-\mathfrak{c}|m-\varphi_{j}|/2},

which verifies (
5.1
). Here, the first inequality follows from (
5.4
), together with the facts that
|u_{j}(k;0)|\leq 1
(as
\bm{u}_{j}
is a unit vector) and
|V_{mk}(s)|\leq 1
(as
\bm{V}(s)
is orthogonal); the second follows from (
5.5
) (with the fact that
T(\log N)^{2}\geq 40T\log N
for sufficiently large
N
) and the fact that we have restricted to the event
\mathsf{E}
from (
5.2
) (with the fact that, if
|m-\varphi_{j}|\geq T(\log N)^{2}
and
|k-m|\leq|\varphi_{j}-m|/2
, then
|k-\varphi_{j}|\geq|m-\varphi_{j}|/2\geq(\log N)^{2}/2
); and the third follows from performing the sums.
∎

5.2.
Resolvent Perturbation Estimates

In this section we estimate the effect of perturbing a random Lax matrix, associated with the open Toda lattice sampled from thermal equilibrium, on its eigenvalues. We first require some notation.

Assumption 5.3
.

Sample
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
under the thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N-1,N}
from
Definition

2.4
, where
\bm{a}=(a_{N_{1}},a_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{N_{2}-1})
and
\bm{b}=(b_{N_{1}},b_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,b_{N_{2}})
. Let
\bm{L}=[L_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
denote the associated Lax matrix (as in
Definition

2.1
), and let
\tilde{\bm{L}}=[\tilde{L}_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
be another tridiagonal matrix. Assume that there is an index set
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
and a real number
\delta\in(0,1)
satisfying

(5.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\notin(\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\mathcal{D})^{2}}|\tilde{L}_{ij}|\leq 2\log N;\qquad\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\mathcal{D}}|L_{ij}-\tilde{L}_{ij}|\leq\delta.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote
\bm{G}(z)=[G_{ij}(z)]=(\bm{L}-z)^{-1}
and
\tilde{\bm{G}}(z)=[\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)]=(\tilde{\bm{L}}-z)^{-1}
.

The following lemma bounds the difference between entries of the resolvents
\bm{G}(z)
and
\tilde{\bm{G}}(z)
, in terms of the distance from their indices to the set
\mathcal{D}
where
\bm{L}
and
\tilde{\bm{L}}
might substantially disagree.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Adopt
Assumption

5.3
; let
\eta\in[\delta,1]
be a real number; and define the set
\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-N\leq\Real z\leq N,\eta\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
. For any integers
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Omega}\big|G_{ij}(z)-\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)\big|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}(\delta^{1/4}+e^{-c\dist(i,\mathcal{D})-c\dist(j,\mathcal{D})}).

Proof.

Throughout, we suppose
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq\dist(j,\mathcal{D})
, as the proof in the alternative case is entirely analogous. We may assume that
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 1
, as otherwise (
5.7
) holds deterministically, by (
3.3
).

We first show a variant of (
5.7
) for a fixed point
z\in\Omega
. So, fix
z_{0}\in\Omega
; abbreviate
G_{km}=G_{km}(z_{0})
and
\tilde{G}_{km}=\tilde{G}_{km}(z_{0})
, for any
k,m\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, also denote

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N),\qquad\text{so that}\qquad\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-1}e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}},

for some constant
\mathfrak{c}>0
, by
Lemma

3.15
. Further let
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\{k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:\dist(k,\mathcal{D})\leq 1\}
. Then, for any real number
s\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(s)>0
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(s)>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|G_{ij}-\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{N_{1}\leq k,m\leq N_{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|G_{ik}(L_{km}-\tilde{L}_{km})\tilde{G}_{kj}|^{s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{-s}\displaystyle\sum_{N_{1}\leq k,m\leq N_{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|G_{ik}|^{s}\cdot|L_{km}-\tilde{L}_{km}|^{s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{s}\eta^{-s}\displaystyle\sum_{k\notin\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{E}[|G_{ik}|^{s}]+\eta^{-s}(3\log N)^{s}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{E}[|G_{ik}|^{s}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\delta^{s}\eta^{-s}\displaystyle\sum_{k\notin\mathcal{D}_{0}}e^{-c_{1}|k-i|}+3C_{1}\eta^{-s}(\log N)^{s}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}e^{-c_{1}|k-i|}

\displaystyle\leq 8c_{1}^{-1}C_{1}\eta^{-s}(\log N)^{s}(\delta^{s}+e^{-c_{1}\dist(i,\mathcal{D}_{0})}),

where in the first inequality we applied (
3.4
); in the second we applied (
3.3
); in the third we used (
5.6
), with the definition (
5.8
) of
\mathsf{E}
(which implies that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|L_{km}-\tilde{L}_{km}|\leq 3\log N
) and the fact that
\bm{L}-\tilde{\bm{L}}
is tridiagonal; in the fourth we used (
3.9
); and in the fifth we performed the sums. Taking
s=2/3
and using the fact that
\Imaginary z_{0}\geq\eta
(as
z_{0}\in\Omega
), we therefore deduce by a Markov estimate that there exist constants
c_{2}>0
and
C_{2}>1
such that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}\big[&\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|G_{ij}-\tilde{G}_{ij}|\geq e^{(\log N)^{2}/2}\eta^{-1}(\delta+e^{-c_{1}\dist(i,\mathcal{D})})^{1/4}\big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}/3}\eta^{2/3}(\delta+e^{-c_{1}\dist(i,\mathcal{D})})^{-1/6}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|G_{ij}-\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2/3}\big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq C_{2}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{2}}(\delta+e^{-c_{1}\dist(i,\mathcal{D})})^{1/2}.\end{aligned}

Now, let us extend the estimate (
5.9
) to simultaneously apply for all
z\in\Omega
. To do this, set
\delta_{0}=(\delta+e^{-c_{1}\dist(i,\mathcal{D})})^{1/4}
. Observe for any
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
with
|z-z^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{0}
that

(5.10)

\displaystyle|G_{ij}(z)-G_{ij}(z^{\prime})|\leq|z-z^{\prime}|\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}|G_{ik}(z)|\cdot|\tilde{G}_{kj}(z)|\leq\eta^{-2}N\delta_{0},

where in the first inequality we used (
3.4
) and in the second we used (
3.3
) (with the fact that
\min\{\Imaginary z,\Imaginary z^{\prime}\}\geq\eta
for
z,z^{\prime}\in\Omega
). Let
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
denote a
\delta_{0}
-mesh of
\Omega
, so that
|\Omega_{0}|\leq 8N\delta_{0}^{-2}
. Denote the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}=\bigg\{\displaystyle\max_{z\in\Omega_{0}}|G_{ij}(z)-\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}/2}\eta^{-1}\delta_{0}\bigg\}.

Applying (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) for all
z_{0}\in\Omega
, yields constants
c_{3}>0
and
C_{3}>1
such that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}[\mathsf{F}^{\complement}]\leq C_{2}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{2}}\delta_{0}^{2}\cdot|\Omega_{0}|+\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]&\leq 8C_{2}Ne^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{2}}+\mathfrak{c}^{-1}e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}\\
&\leq C_{3}e^{-c_{3}(\log N)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

Observe for sufficiently large
N
that
|G_{ij}(z)-\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0}
holds for all
z\in\Omega
on the event
\mathsf{F}
, due to (
5.10
) and the estimate
e^{(\log N)^{2}/2}\eta^{-1}\delta_{0}+2\eta^{-2}N\delta_{0}\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0}
. Since
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq\dist(j,\mathcal{D})
, this yields (
5.11
).
∎

Using
Lemma

5.4
and
Lemma

3.7
, we can establish the following two corollaries. The first indicates that eigenvalues of
\bm{L}
with localization centers distant from
\mathcal{D}
are also nearly eigenvalues of
\tilde{\bm{L}}
(with the same localization center), and the second essentially indicates the reverse.

Corollary 5.5
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Adopt
Assumption

5.3
; assume
\delta\leq e^{-10(\log N)^{2}}
, and let
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
be a real number. Fix
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
, and let
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote a
\zeta
-localization center of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}
, satisfying

(5.12)

\displaystyle\dist(\varphi,\mathcal{D})\geq(\log N)^{3}.

Then there exists an eigenvalue
\tilde{\lambda}\in\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}
such that

(5.13)

\displaystyle|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}(\delta^{1/8}+e^{-c\dist(\varphi,\mathcal{D})}),

and
\varphi
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center for
\tilde{\lambda}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,1)
denote the constant
c
from
Lemma

5.4
, and set

(5.14)

\displaystyle\delta_{0}=\delta^{1/4}+e^{-\mathfrak{c}\dist(\varphi,\mathcal{D})};\qquad\eta=(e^{(\log N)^{2}}\delta_{0})^{1/2}.

Recalling the event
\mathsf{E}
from (
5.8
), also define event

(5.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{F}&=\mathsf{E}\cap\bigg\{\displaystyle\sup_{E\in[-N,N]}\big|G_{\varphi\varphi}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)-\tilde{G}_{\varphi\varphi}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)\big|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0}\bigg\}.\end{aligned}

By (
5.6
) and the min-max principle, on
\mathsf{F}
, we have
\eig\bm{L}\cup\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}\subseteq[-N,N]
for sufficiently large
N
.

By (
5.8
) and
Lemma

5.4
, we may restrict to the e vent
\mathsf{F}
. We then apply
Lemma

3.7
, with the parameters
(\varphi;\eta,\zeta,\delta;\bm{A},\bm{B})
there equal to
(\varphi;\eta,\zeta,e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0};\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}})
here. To verify (
3.5
), observe that the first estimate there follows for sufficiently large
N
from the fact that

(5.16)

\displaystyle(2\eta)\cdot e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0}\leq 2e^{(\log N)^{2}/2}\cdot(\delta^{1/4}+e^{-\mathfrak{c}\dist(\varphi,\mathcal{D})})^{1/2}\leq e^{-400(\log N)^{3/2}}\leq\zeta^{2},

where in the first bound we used (
5.14
); in the second we used the facts that
\delta\leq e^{-10(\log N)^{2}}
, that
\dist(\varphi,\mathcal{D})\geq(\log N)^{3}
, and that
N
is sufficiently large; and in the third we used the fact that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. The second estimate in (
3.5
) follows from the fact that
\varphi
is a
\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}
, and the third follows from our restriction to
\mathsf{F}
.

Thus,
Lemma

3.7
yields some
\tilde{\lambda}\in\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}
such that
|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\leq 3N\zeta^{-2}\eta
, which by (
5.14
) (and again the fact that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
) confirms (
5.13
). Since
(6N)^{-1/2}\zeta\geq N^{-1}\zeta
,
Lemma

3.7
also indicates that
\varphi
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
\tilde{\lambda}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}
, establishing the corollary.
∎

Corollary 5.6
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Adopt
Assumption

5.3
; assume that
\delta\leq e^{-10(\log N)^{2}}
; and let
\zeta\geq Ne^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
be a real number. Fix
\tilde{\lambda}\in\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}
, and let
\tilde{\varphi}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote a
\zeta
-localization center of
\tilde{\lambda}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}
; suppose that

(5.17)

\displaystyle\dist(\tilde{\varphi},\mathcal{D})\geq(\log N)^{3}.

(1)

There exists a unique eigenvalue
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
such that
|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}(\delta^{1/8}+e^{-c\dist(\tilde{\varphi},\mathcal{D})})
.

(2)

We have that
\tilde{\varphi}
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}
, and any
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
satisfies
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq(\log N)^{2}/2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,1)
denote the constant
c
from
Lemma

5.4
, and set
\delta_{0}
and
\eta
as in (
5.14
). Recalling
Definition

3.13
and the event
\mathsf{F}
from (
5.15
), define the event
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{F}\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}/2})
. By (
5.8
),
Lemma

3.18
, and
Lemma

5.4
,
\mathsf{G}
is overwhelmingly probable. By (
5.6
) and the min-max principle we have on
\mathsf{G}
that
\eig\bm{L}\cup\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}\subseteq[-N,N]
for sufficiently large
N
.

Restricting to the event
\mathsf{G}
, we then apply
Lemma

3.7
, with the parameters
(\varphi;\eta,\zeta,\delta;\bm{A},\bm{B})
there equal to
(\tilde{\varphi};\eta,\zeta,e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}\delta_{0};\tilde{\bm{L}},\bm{L})
here. As in the proof of
Corollary

5.5
, the first bound in (
3.5
) is verified by (
5.16
); the second by the fact that
\tilde{\varphi}
is a
\zeta
-localization center for
\tilde{\lambda}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}
; and the third by our restriction to
\mathsf{G}
. Thus,
Lemma

3.7
gives some
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
such that
|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\leq 3N\zeta^{-2}\eta
, which by (
5.14
) (and again the bound
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
) yields some
\lambda
satisfying the first statement of the corollary. As any two distinct eigenvalues of
\bm{L}
differ by at least
e^{-(\log N)^{2}/2}
by our restriction to
\mathsf{G}
, and
6N\zeta^{-2}\eta\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
(by (
5.14
), (
5.17
), and the facts that
\zeta\geq e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
and
\delta\leq e^{-10(\log N)^{2}}
), such an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
satisfying these hypotheses is unique.

Since
(6N)^{-1/2}\zeta\geq N^{-1}\zeta
,
Lemma

3.7
also indicates that
\varphi
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
\tilde{\lambda}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}
. Thus, the second part of
Lemma

5.1
yields that, with overwhelming probability, any
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}
satisfies
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq(\log N)^{2}/2
. This confirms the second statement of the corollary.
∎

6.
Eigenvector Analysis for Lax Matrices

In this section we analyze the eigenvectors of random Lax matrices. We begin in
Section

6.1
by proving a result,
Proposition

6.1
, that approximates the exponential rate of decay of the first entries of these eigenvectors, by reducing it to
Proposition

6.3
below; the latter indicates that eigenvalues of the Lax matrix are close to those of certain truncations of it. We then show
Proposition

6.3
in
Section

6.2
and
Section

6.3
. Throughout this section, we adopt
Assumption

2.8
.

6.1.
Eigenvector Decay for Lax Matrices

The following proposition estimates the behavior of the first entries of eigenvectors of the Lax matrix
\bm{L}(t)
. Recall that we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any real number
t\in[0,T]
, the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any integer
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying

(6.1)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+N^{1/100}\leq\varphi_{t}(k)\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}-N^{1/100},

we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|-\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{t}(k)-1}\log L_{j,j+1}(t)+\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\Bigg|\leq(\log N)^{6}.

To establish
Proposition

6.1
, we use the following lower bounds on
\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|
and
\log|u_{k}(N_{2};t)|
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Adopting the notation and assumptions of
Proposition

6.1
, we have with overwhelming probability that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{t}(k)-1}\log L_{j,j+1}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{6};\\
&\log|u_{k}(N_{2};t)|\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j=\varphi_{t}(k)}^{N_{2}-1}\log L_{j,j+1}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)>\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{6}.\end{aligned}

Given
Lemma

6.2
, we can quickly establish
Proposition

6.1
using
Lemma

3.8
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

Let
\mathsf{F}
denote the event on which (
6.3
) holds. By
Lemma

6.2
,
\mathsf{F}
is overwhelmingly probable, so it suffices to show that (
6.2
) holds for sufficiently large
N
on the event
\mathsf{F}
; assume to the contrary that this is false. Since the first bound in (
6.3
) holds but (
6.2
) does not hold, we must have

\displaystyle\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{t}(k)-1}\log L_{j,j+1}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|+(\log N)^{6}.

Together with the second bound in (
6.3
), this yields

\displaystyle\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|+\log|u_{k}(N_{2};t)|\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}\log L_{j,j+1}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq k}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{6},

which contradicts
Lemma

3.8
. This verifies the proposition.
∎

To prove
Lemma

6.2
, we will make use of
Lemma

3.9
, which expresses the eigenvector entries of
\bm{L}(t)
in terms of transfer matrices. The latter admit an explicit form, given by
Lemma

3.10
, which involves the eigenvalues of truncations of
\bm{L}(t)
. So, we require an estimate on how the eigenvalues of the Lax matrix
\bm{L}(t)
change after setting one of its rows and columns to
0
. This is provided by the first part of the following proposition. Its second part states that the localization centers of these eigenvalues cannot differ by too much, and its third improves the bound on this difference (making it independent of
T
) if one of the localization centers is not too close to an endpoint of the domain
\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. The proof of this proposition will appear in
Section

6.3
below.

Proposition 6.3
.

For any real number
t\in[0,T]
, the following holds with overwhelming probability, for some constant
c>0
. Let
\ell\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
be an integer, and assume that

(6.4)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{4}+N^{1/100}\leq\ell\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{4}-N^{1/100}.

Set
\bm{P}=\bm{L}(t)
and
\bm{M}=\bm{P}^{(\ell)}
. Let
\mu\in\eig\bm{M}
be any eigenvalue of
\bm{M}
; let
\Phi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
be any
\zeta
-localization center for
\mu
with respect to
\bm{M}
. Suppose that
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
.

(1)

There exists a unique eigenvalue
\lambda\in\eig\bm{P}
such that
|\lambda-\mu|\leq e^{-c\min\{|\Phi-\ell|,N^{1/100}\}}
.

(2)

If
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
is a
\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
, then we have
|\varphi-\Phi|\leq T(\log N)^{3}
.

(3)

Assume that

(6.5)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{1/100}\leq\Phi\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{1/100}.

If
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
is a
\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
, then
|\varphi-\Phi|\leq(\log N)^{2}
.

Given
Proposition

6.3
, we can establish
Lemma

6.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

We only establish the first bound in (
6.3
), as the second would then follow from symmetry. Throughout, we set
\bm{P}=[P_{ij}]=\bm{L}(t)
and
v_{j}=u_{k}(j;t)
for each
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
.

Recall from
Section

3.4
the notation on the transfer matrices
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{K}}
, defined by (
3.4
) and (
3.8
); abbreviate
\bm{S}=[S_{ij}]=\bm{S}_{\llbracket N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)\rrbracket}(\lambda_{k};\bm{P})\in\Mat_{2\times 2}
, whose entries are indexed by
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Also denote
\bm{Q}=\bm{P}^{[N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)-1]}
, where we recall from above
Lemma

3.10
that this is the matrix obtained by restricting
\bm{P}
to rows and columns indexed by
\llbracket N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)-1\rrbracket
. Then
Lemma

3.9
yields
\bm{S}\cdot(0,v_{N_{1}})=(v_{\varphi_{t}(k)},v_{\varphi_{t}(k)+1})
, so
S_{12}\cdot v_{N_{1}}=v_{\varphi_{t}(k)}
. We thus deduce for sufficiently large
N
that

\displaystyle\log|v_{N_{1}}|

\displaystyle=\log|v_{\varphi_{t}(k)}|-\log|S_{12}|

\displaystyle\geq\log\zeta-\log|S_{12}|\geq\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{t}(k)-1}\log P_{j,j+1}-\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}}\log|\mu-\lambda_{k}|-(\log N)^{2},

where in the second statement we used the fact that
\varphi_{t}
is a
\zeta
-localization center bijection for
\bm{P}
, and in the third we used
Lemma

3.10
and the fact that
\zeta\geq e^{-100(\log N)^{3/2}}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
. To verify the first bound in (
6.3
), it therefore suffices to show that with overwhelming probability we have

(6.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}}\log|\mu-\lambda_{k}|\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot(\log N)^{6}.

To that end, recalling
Definition

3.13
, define the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{P}}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{P}}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
. Then
\mathsf{E}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable, due to
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

3.18
.

We next apply
Proposition

6.3
, with the
\ell
there equal to
\varphi_{t}(k)
here; observe that the estimate (
6.4
) assumed in that proposition holds by (
6.1
). Thus,
Proposition

6.3
yields an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, on which the following holds. Let
\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}
be any eigenvalue that admits a
\zeta
-localization center
\Phi\in\llbracket N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)-1\rrbracket
with respect to
\bm{Q}
satisfying
\Phi\leq\varphi_{t}(k)-(\log N)^{4}
.

(1)

There exists a unique eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda(\mu)\in\eig\bm{P}
such that
|\lambda-\mu|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
.

(2)

Letting
\lambda=\lambda_{\psi}
for some
\psi\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, we have
|\varphi_{t}(\psi)-\Phi|\leq T(\log N)^{3}
.

(3)

If
\Phi\geq N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3}+N^{1/100}/2
, then we further have
|\varphi_{t}(\psi)-\Phi|\leq(\log N)^{2}
.

In what follows, we restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}
and show (
6.6
) holds.

To that end, denote
\eig\bm{Q}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{\varphi_{t}(k)-N_{1}})
, and let
\Phi:\llbracket 1,\varphi_{t}(k)-N_{1}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)-1\rrbracket
denote a
(2N)^{-1}
-localization center bijection for
\bm{Q}
, which is guaranteed to exist by
Lemma

2.7
. Let
\mathcal{J}
denote the set of indices
j\in\llbracket 1,\varphi_{t}(k)-N_{1}\rrbracket
such that
\Phi(j)\leq\varphi_{t}(k)-(\log N)^{4}
. For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, let
\psi_{j}\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
denote the unique index such that

(6.7)

\displaystyle|\lambda_{\psi_{j}}-\mu_{\Phi(j)}|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}},\qquad\text{where $j$ satisfies}\qquad\varphi_{t}(\psi_{j})<\varphi_{t}(k).

Such an index
\psi_{j}
satisfying the first bound in (
6.7
) exists and is unique by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
. To verify that
\psi_{j}
satisfies the second observe that, if
\Phi(j)\geq N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3}+N^{1/100}/2
, then

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(\psi_{j})\leq\Phi(j)+(\log N)^{2}\leq\varphi_{t}(k)-(\log N)^{4}+(\log N)^{2}<\varphi_{t}(k),

the first bound since we restricted to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, the second since
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and the third since
N>1
. If instead
\Phi(j)<N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3}+N^{1/100}/2
, then

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(\psi_{j})\leq\Phi(j)+T(\log N)^{3}\leq N_{1}+2T(\log N)^{4}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{1/100}<\varphi_{t}(k),

the first bound by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, the second by our assumption on
\Phi(j)
, and the third by (
6.1
). This confirms (
6.7
). We also have that

(6.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\log|\mu_{h}-\lambda_{k}|\leq\log N\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for all $h\in\llbracket 1,\varphi_{t}(k)-N_{1}-1z\rrbracket$};\\
&\log|\mu_{j}-\lambda_{k}|\leq\log|\lambda_{\psi_{j}}-\lambda_{k}|+e^{-(\log N)^{2}},\qquad\text{for all $j\in\mathcal{J}$}.\end{aligned}

Here, the first bound holds since
|\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i}|\leq 2\log N
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
and the fact that
\mathsf{E}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{Q}}(\log N)
, by
Remark

3.14
); the second holds since
|\lambda_{\psi_{j}}-\mu_{j}|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
, with the bound
|\lambda_{\psi_{j}}-\lambda_{k}|\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
and the fact from (
6.7
) that
\psi_{j}\neq k
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\bm{Q}}\log|\mu-\lambda_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\log|\mu_{h}-\lambda_{k}|+(\log N)^{5}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\log|\lambda_{\psi_{j}}-\lambda_{k}|+2(\log N)^{5}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|+3(\log N)^{5}.

Here, in the first estimate, we used the fact that at most
(\log N)^{4}
indices
h\notin\mathcal{J}
exist, with the first bound in (
6.8
) for each such
h
. In the second, we used the second bound in (
6.8
). In the third we used the fact that
\varphi_{t}(\psi_{j})<\varphi_{t}(k)
for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
(by (
6.7
)); the bound
\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\leq\log(2\log N)\leq\log N
for all indices
i
with
\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1},\varphi_{t}(k)-1\rrbracket
but that are not of the form
\psi_{j}
for some
j\in\mathcal{J}
; and the fact that at most
(\log N)^{4}
such indices
i
exist (as is quickly verified from the injectivity of
\varphi_{t}
and
\psi
, the second statement in (
6.7
), and the fact that there are at most
(\log N)^{4}
indices not in
\mathcal{J}
). This establishes the second bound in (
6.6
) and thus the lemma.
∎

6.2.
Eigenvalues of Truncated Lax Matrices

In this section we begin the proof of
Proposition

6.3
; we adopt the notation of that proposition throughout. We first address it when (
6.5
) holds, that is, when
\Phi
is not too close to
N_{1}
or
N_{2}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Proposition
6.3
holds assuming (
6.5
).

To prove
Proposition

6.4
, we first apply
Proposition

4.4
, which yields a random matrix
\bm{Q}=[Q_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
with the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
, and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}
, on which we have

(6.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|P_{ij}-Q_{ij}|\leq e^{-K/5},\qquad\text{for any $K\geq T\log N$}.

Lemma 6.5
.

If (
6.5
) holds, then there exists a constant
c>0
such that the following two statements hold with overwhelming probability.

(1)

There exists an eigenvalue
\kappa\in\eig\bm{Q}
such that
|\mu-\kappa|\leq e^{-c\min\{|\Phi-\ell|,N^{1/100}\}}
.

(2)

The index
\Phi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\kappa
with respect to
\bm{Q}
.

Proof.

Define
\bm{R}=[R_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
by setting
\bm{R}=\bm{Q}^{(\ell)}
. Throughout this proof, we assume that
\Phi\leq\ell
, as the proof when
\Phi\geq\ell
is entirely analogous. Then denote by
\bm{P}^{\prime}=[P_{ij}^{\prime}]
and
\bm{Q}^{\prime}=[Q_{ij}^{\prime}]
the top
(\ell-N_{1})\times(\ell-N_{1})
corners of
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
, respectively. In this way, their rows and columns are indexed by
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},\ell-1\rrbracket
, and we have
P_{ij}^{\prime}=P_{ij}=M_{ij}
and
Q_{ij}^{\prime}=Q_{ij}=R_{ij}
for each such
(i,j)
; observe that
\eig\bm{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\eig\bm{M}
and
\eig\bm{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\eig\bm{R}
. Since
\Phi\leq\ell
, we also have
\mu\in\eig\bm{P}^{\prime}
.

To establish the lemma, we will first use
Corollary

5.6
to show
\mu
is close to some
\nu\in\eig\bm{R}
. To that end, recalling
Definition

3.13
, define the quantities
K,\delta>0
and event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
by

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &K=\displaystyle\frac{1}{10}\cdot\min\{|\Phi-\ell|,N^{1/100}\}+\frac{T}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{3};\\
&\quad\delta=e^{-K/4};\quad\mathsf{E}_{1}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(s)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\log N}{600}\Big).\end{aligned}

By
Lemma

3.15
,
\mathsf{E}_{1}
holds with overwhelming probability, so we restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows.

Applying
Corollary

5.6
requires (through
Assumption

5.3
) a bound on
|P_{ij}^{\prime}-Q_{ij}^{\prime}|
for
i,j
sufficiently far from
N_{1}
. This will follow from (
6.9
); indeed, observe that
N_{2}-\ell\geq T(\log N)^{4}+N^{1/100}\geq K
, where the first bound holds by (
6.4
) and the second holds by (
6.10
). Thus
N_{2}-K\geq\ell
, so since
(P_{ij}^{\prime},Q_{ij}^{\prime})=(P_{ij},Q_{ij})
for
i,j\leq\ell
, it follows from (
6.9
) that

(6.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,\ell\rrbracket}|P_{ij}^{\prime}-Q_{ij}^{\prime}|\leq\delta.

Using this, we next verify
Assumption

5.3
with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}})
there given by
(\bm{Q}^{\prime},\bm{P}^{\prime})
here. To that end, observe that
|P_{ij}|\leq(\log N)/50\leq 2\log(\ell-N_{1})
, where the first bound follows from
Lemma

3.5
and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, and the second follows from (
6.4
). This, together with (
6.11
) and the fact that
\bm{Q}
has the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
, implies that
Assumption

5.3
holds, with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}};\mathcal{D};N)
there equal to
(\bm{Q}^{\prime},\bm{P}^{\prime};\llbracket N_{1},N_{1}+K-1\rrbracket;\ell-N_{1})
here. Since (
6.5
) and (
6.10
) together yield

(6.12)

\displaystyle\Phi-N_{1}\geq T(\log N)^{3}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{1/100}\geq 2K+(\log N)^{3},

we deduce that
\Phi\geq N_{1}+K+(\log N)^{3}
, verifying (
5.17
). Since
\mu\in\eig\bm{P}^{\prime}
, the first statement of
Corollary

5.6
yields a constant
c_{1}>0
such that, with overwhelming probability, there exists an eigenvalue
\nu\in\eig\bm{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\eig\bm{R}
satisfying

(6.13)

\displaystyle|\nu-\mu|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}(\delta^{1/16}+e^{-2c_{1}|\Phi-N_{1}-K|})\leq e^{-c_{1}K},

where in the last inequality we used (
6.12
) and the bound
K\geq(\log N)^{4}/10
(as
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
). The second statement of
Corollary

5.6
further impies that
\Phi
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center of
\nu
with respect to
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
, and thus with respect to
\bm{R}
. We further restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
in what follows.

We now use
Corollary

5.6
again to show that
\nu
is close to some
\kappa\in\eig\bm{Q}
; this will proceed similarly to above. Since
R_{ij}=Q_{ij}
unless
\ell\in\{i,j\}
(and
\bm{Q}
has the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
), we have by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}\cap\mathsf{E}_{1}
that
Assumption

5.3
holds with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}};\delta;\mathcal{D})
there equal to
(\bm{Q},\bm{R};0;\{\ell\})
here. Since
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
, the first statement of
Corollary

5.6
yields a constant
c_{2}>0
such that, with overwhelming probability, there exists an eigenvalue
\kappa\in\eig\bm{Q}
such that

(6.14)

\displaystyle|\kappa-\nu|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\cdot e^{-2c_{2}|\Phi-\ell|}\leq e^{-c_{2}|\Phi-\ell|},

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
. Thus, (
6.13
) and (
6.14
) imply the first statement of the lemma. The second statement of
Corollary

5.6
further implies that
\Phi
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\kappa
with respect to
\bm{Q}
, verifying the second statement of the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.4
.

Throughout this proof, we adopt the notation and assumptions from
Lemma

6.5
and its proof. In particular, we recall the quantities
K,\delta>0
and the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
from (
6.10
). Recalling
Definition

3.13
, we further define the event

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{1}=\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{P}}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{Q}}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}}).

By
Lemma

2.2
,
Lemma

3.18
, and a union bound,
\mathsf{F}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable. We restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{F}_{1}
, and further to the event that
Lemma

6.5
holds, in what follows.

We will use
Corollary

5.5
to show that
\kappa
is close to some
\lambda\in\eig\bm{P}
. To that end, first observe from (
6.9
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}\cap\mathsf{E}_{1}
(with the fact that
\bm{Q}
has the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
) that
Assumption

5.3
holds, with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}};\mathcal{D})
there equal to
(\bm{Q},\bm{P};\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}+K\rrbracket)
here. Also, by (
6.5
),
\min\{\Phi-N_{1}-K,N_{2}-\Phi-K\}\geq N^{1/100}/2\geq(\log N)^{3}
, which verifies (
5.12
). Therefore,
Corollary

5.5
applies and yields a constant
c_{1}>0
and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{F}_{2}
, on which the following holds. There exists
\lambda\in\eig\bm{P}
such that
\Phi
is a
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
, and

(6.16)

\displaystyle|\lambda-\kappa|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}(\delta^{1/8}+e^{-2c_{1}\min\{\Phi-N_{1}-K,N_{2}-K-\Phi\}})\leq e^{-c_{1}K},

where in the last inequality we used the facts that
\min\{\Phi-N_{1},N_{2}-\Phi\}\geq T(\log N)^{3}+N^{1/100}/2\geq 2K+(\log N)^{3}
and
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
. Together with the first statement in
Lemma

6.5
, this implies the existence of
\lambda
satisfying the conditions in the first statement of
Proposition

6.3
, upon additionally restricting to
\mathsf{F}_{2}
. The uniqueness of such a
\lambda\in\eig\bm{P}
follows follows from our restriction to the event
\mathsf{F}_{1}
from (
6.15
) (with the fact that
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
).

It remains to establish the third part of the proposition (which implies the second, as we have assumed that (
6.5
) holds), to which we must use
Corollary

5.6
(instead of
Corollary

5.5
). Fix any
\zeta
-localization center
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
. Let a
\bm{u}(t)=(u(N_{1};t),u(N_{1}+1;t),\ldots,u(N_{2};t))\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a unit eigenvector of
\bm{P}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
. Then, by the definition of
\varphi
and
Corollary

5.5
, we have

(6.17)

\displaystyle|u(\varphi;t)|\geq\zeta,\qquad\text{and}\qquad|u(\Phi;t)|\geq N^{-3}\zeta.

We must show (
5.17
), to which end we claim that, with high probability,

(6.18)

\displaystyle\min\{\varphi-N_{1},N_{2}-\varphi\}\geq K+(\log N)^{3}.

To do so, observe that
Lemma

5.2
yields an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{F}_{3}
, on which the following holds. An index
m\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
can only be a
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
if
|m-\varphi|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
. Restricting to
\mathsf{F}_{3}
, the second bound in (
6.17
) then implies that we must have
|\Phi-\varphi|<T(\log N)^{2}
for sufficiently large
N
. Together with the estimate (by (
6.5
) and (
6.10
))

\displaystyle\min\{\Phi-N_{1},N_{2}-\Phi\}

\displaystyle\geq T(\log N)^{3}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{1/100}\geq K+T(\log N)^{2}+(\log N)^{3},

this confirms (
6.18
).

The bound (
6.18
) (with (
6.9
)) verifies (
5.17
), with the parameters
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}};\tilde{\lambda},\tilde{\varphi};\delta;\mathcal{D})
there equal to
(\bm{Q},\bm{P};\lambda,\varphi;\delta;\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket)
here. Thus,
Corollary

5.6
applies and yields a constant
c_{2}>0
such that, with overwhelming probability, there exists
\kappa^{\prime}\in\eig\bm{Q}
with

\displaystyle|\kappa^{\prime}-\lambda|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}(\delta^{1/8}+e^{-c_{2}\min\{\varphi-N_{1}-K,N_{2}-K-\varphi\}})\leq e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}},

where in the last inequality we used the definition (
6.10
) of
\delta
(with the fact that
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
) with (
6.18
). Together with (
6.16
), the fact that
|\Phi-\ell|\geq(\log N)^{4}
, and our restriction to the event
\mathsf{F}_{1}
from (
6.15
), this implies that
\kappa=\kappa^{\prime}
. Hence, the second part of
Corollary

5.6
implies that
\varphi
is a
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center for
\kappa
with respect to
\bm{Q}
. Since
\Phi
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\kappa
with respect to
\bm{Q}
(by
Lemma

6.5
), it also implies that
|\varphi-\Phi|\leq(\log N)^{2}
, verifying the third (and thus also the second) statement of the proposition.
∎

6.3.
Proof of
Proposition

6.3

In this section we establish
Proposition

6.3
, adopting its notation and assumptions throughout. For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote the resolvents of
\bm{M}
and
\bm{P}
by
\breve{\bm{G}}(z)=[\breve{G}_{ij}(z)]=(\bm{M}-z)^{-1}
and
\check{\bm{G}}(z)=[\check{G}_{ij}(z)]=(\bm{P}-z)^{-1}
, respectively. The following lemma estimates the
(\Phi,\Phi)
entry of
\check{\bm{G}}-\breve{\bm{G}}
, assuming that (
6.5
) does not hold. Its proof is similar to that of
Lemma

5.4
(by using
Lemma

5.2
in place of
Lemma

3.16
), so we only outline it.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists a constant
c>0
so that the following holds with overwhleming probability. Suppose that (
6.5
) does not hold, and let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
e^{-cN^{1/100}}\leq\eta\leq 1
. Denoting
\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-N\leq\Real z\leq N,\eta\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
, we have

(6.19)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Omega}\big|\check{G}_{\Phi\Phi}(z)-\breve{G}_{\Phi\Phi}(z)\big|\leq e^{-cN^{1/100}}.

Proof of
Lemma

6.6
(Outline).

Throughout this proof, recalling
Definition

3.13
, we set

(6.20)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{P}}(\log N),\qquad\text{so that}\qquad\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{F}^{\complement}]\leq c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{2}},

for some constant
c_{1}\in(0,1)
, by
Lemma

3.15
. We begin by using
Lemma

5.2
to estimate the eigenvectors of
\bm{P}
. To that end, first observe, by
Lemma

2.2
and the fact that
\bm{P}=\bm{L}(t)
, that
\eig\bm{P}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{N})
. For each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, recall that
\bm{u}_{j}(t)=(u_{j}(N_{1};t),u_{j}(N_{1}+1;t),\ldots,u_{j}(N_{2};t))\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
denotes the nonnegatively normalized, unit eigenvector of
\bm{P}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
. For each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, also let
\psi_{j}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote a
N^{-1/2}
-localization center for the eigenvector
\bm{u}_{j}
of
\bm{L}
of eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
(which is guaranteed to exist, as
\bm{u}
is a unit vector). Since (
6.5
) does not hold, (
6.4
) implies for sufficiently large
N
that

\displaystyle|\ell-\Phi|\geq K_{0},\qquad\text{where}\qquad K_{0}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big(T(\log N)^{4}+N^{1/100}\big).

Thus, for any
i\in\{\ell-1,\ell,\ell+1\}
and
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, we either have
|\Phi-\psi_{j}|\geq K_{0}/2\geq T(\log N)^{2}
or
|i-\psi_{j}|\geq K_{0}/2\geq T(\log N)^{2}
. Therefore,
Lemma

5.2
yields a constant
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,c_{1}/25)
and an event
\mathsf{E}_{0}
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{0}^{\complement}]\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-1}e^{-25\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
, such that

(6.21)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{0}}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i:|i-\ell|\leq 1}\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}|u_{k}(\Phi;t)|\cdot|u_{k}(i;t)|\leq N\cdot e^{-25\mathfrak{c}K_{0}}\leq e^{-12\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}.

Set
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{0}\cap\mathsf{F}
, which satisfies
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-1}e^{-20\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
, by (
6.20
) and a union bound.

Now, let us show that (
6.19
) holds with high probability for a fixed point
z\in\Omega
. Fix
z_{0}\in\Omega
; abbreviate
\check{G}_{ij}=\check{G}_{ij}(z_{0})
and
\breve{G}_{ij}=\breve{G}_{ij}(z_{0})
for each
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. For
\eta\geq e^{-\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
, we have

(6.22)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|\check{G}_{\Phi\Phi}-\breve{G}_{\Phi\Phi}|\big]&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:|i-\ell|\leq 1\\
j:|j-\ell|\leq 1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|\check{G}_{\Phi i}\cdot P_{ij}\cdot\breve{G}_{j\Phi}|\big]\\
&\leq 9\eta^{-1}\cdot\log N\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i:|i-\ell|\leq 1}\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\cdot|\check{G}_{\Phi i}|]\\
&\leq 9\eta^{-2}\cdot\log N\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i:|i-\ell|\leq 1}\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\big[|u_{k}(\Phi;t)|\cdot|u_{k}(i;t)|\big]\\
&\leq 9\eta^{-2}\cdot\log N\cdot e^{-12\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}\leq e^{-9\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}},\end{aligned}

where the first inequality follows from (
3.4
), together with the fact that
P_{ij}-M_{ij}\in\{P_{ij},0\}
with
P_{ij}-M_{ij}=0
unless
i,j\in\{\ell-1,\ell,\ell+1\}
; the second from (
3.3
) (with the fact that
\Imaginary z_{0}\geq\eta
, as
z_{0}\in\Omega
) and the definition (
6.20
) of
\mathsf{F}
; the third from (
3.2
), together with the bound
|\lambda_{k}-z_{0}|\geq\Imaginary z_{0}\geq\eta
for any
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
; the fourth from (
6.21
); and the fifth from the fact that
\eta\geq e^{-\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
. The estimate (
6.22
), together with a Markov bound, verifies (
6.19
) at
z=z_{0}
.

To extend it to all
z\in\Omega
, we first use a union bound to apply (
6.22
) on an
e^{-3\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
-mesh
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
. Then, using (
3.4
) and (
3.3
), we approximate the resolvents of
\bm{M}
and
\bm{P}
at an arbitrary points
z\in\Omega
by those at the nearest point
z_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. This is very similar to what was done at the end of the proof of
Lemma

5.4
, so we omit further details.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

In what follows, we assume that (
6.5
) does not hold, as
Proposition

6.4
establishes the proposition when it does. Letting
\mathfrak{c}>0
denote the constant
c/10
from
Lemma

6.6
, set
\eta=e^{-2\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
and define the event (recalling
Definition

3.13
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{P}}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{P}}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})

\displaystyle\qquad\cap\bigg\{\displaystyle\sup_{E\in[-N,N]}\big|\check{G}_{\Phi\Phi}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)-\breve{G}_{\Phi\Phi}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)\big|\leq e^{-10\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}\bigg\}.

By
Lemma

3.15
,
Lemma

3.18
(with
Lemma

2.2
), and
Lemma

6.6
,
\mathsf{G}
is overwhelmingly probable.

Restricting to
\mathsf{G}_{1}
, we next apply
Lemma

3.7
with the parameters
(\lambda,\varphi;\eta,\zeta,\delta;\bm{A},\bm{B})
there equal to
(\mu,\Phi;\eta,\zeta,e^{-10\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}};\bm{M},\bm{P})
here. The first bound in (
3.5
) holds for sufficiently large
N
by the facts that
\zeta\geq e^{-100(\log N)^{3/2}}
and
\eta=e^{-2\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
; the second holds since
\Phi
is a
\zeta
-localization center of
\mu
with respect to
\bm{M}
; and the third holds by our restriction to
\mathsf{G}_{1}
. Hence,
Lemma

3.7
yields an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\eig\bm{P}
such that
|\lambda-\mu|\leq 3N\zeta^{-2}\eta\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
(again as
\zeta\geq e^{-100(\log N)^{3/2}}
and
\eta=e^{-2\mathfrak{c}N^{1/100}}
). This shows the first statement of the proposition; it remains to verify the second.

To that end, observe that
Lemma

3.7
further indicates that
\Phi
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
. Letting
\Psi\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}
,
Lemma

5.2
implies that the following holds with overwhelming probability. We have
|m-\Psi|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
for any
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
m\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{P}
. Applying this for
m\in\{\Phi,\varphi\}
then yields
|\varphi-\Phi|\leq 2T(\log N)^{2}\leq T(\log N)^{3}
for sufficiently large
N
; this confirms the second statement of the proposition.
∎

7.
Properties of Localization Centers

We establish
Proposition

2.9
in
Section

7.1
and
Proposition

2.10
in
Section

7.3
, after showing several estimates on spacings between particles in the Toda lattice in
Section

7.2
. Throughout this section, we adopt
Assumption

2.8
.

7.1.
Proof of
Proposition

2.9

In this section we show
Proposition

2.9
by proving the following generalization of it. Recall we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Proposition 7.1
.

Assume more generally that
\zeta\geq N^{3}e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. For any real number
\mathfrak{d}>0
, there exists a constant
c=c(\mathfrak{d})>0
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Fix real numbers
t,\tilde{t}\in[0,T]
; an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
; and
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization centers
\varphi,\tilde{\varphi}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(t)
and
\bm{L}(\tilde{t})
, respectively. If
\varphi
satisfies (
2.9
) and
|t-\tilde{t}|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{d}(\log N)^{2}}
holds, then
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq(\log N)^{3}
.

Proof.

We may assume in what follows that
T\geq 3
and that
\mathfrak{d}
is sufficiently small, in a way to be determined later. We first restrict to several events.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq[0,T]
denote an
e^{-\mathfrak{d}(\log N)^{2}}
-mesh of
[0,T]
, and fix
s\in\mathcal{T}
. By
Proposition

4.4
, there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
; an event
\mathsf{E}_{1}(s)
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{1}(s)^{\complement}]\leq c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{2}}
; and a random matrix
\bm{M}(s)=[M_{ij}(s)]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
with the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
, such that the following holds on
\mathsf{E}_{1}(s)
. Denoting
N_{1}^{\prime}=N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5/2}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}=N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5/2}
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}|M_{ij}(s)-L_{ij}(s)|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{5/2}/5}.

Corollary
5.6
(with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}};\mathcal{D};\delta;\tilde{\lambda})
there equal to
(\bm{M},\bm{L}(s);\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket;e^{-(\log N)^{5/2}/5};\lambda)
here) therefore yields a constant
c_{2}>0
and an event
\mathsf{E}_{2}(s)
for each
s\in\mathcal{T}
, with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{2}(s)^{\complement}]\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{2}}
, such that the following holds on
\mathsf{E}_{2}(s)
. There exists a unique eigenvalue
\mu=\mu(s)\in\eig\bm{M}(s)
so that, for any
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center
\varphi_{s}\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+(\log N)^{3},N_{2}^{\prime}-(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(s)
and any
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center
\psi_{s}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
for
\mu(s)
with respect to
\bm{M}(s)
, we have
|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|\leq(\log N)^{2}
.

Set
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{d}/8
. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, let
\mathsf{E}_{3}=\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}})
, so
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

3.18
give a constant
c_{3}=c_{3}(\mathfrak{d})>0
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{3}^{\complement}]\leq c_{3}^{-1}e^{-c_{3}(\log N)^{2}}
. Further let
\mathsf{E}_{4}
denote the event on which
Lemma

5.2
holds, which satisfies
