\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{4}^{\complement}]\leq c_{4}^{-1}e^{-c_{4}(\log N)^{2}}
for some
c_{4}>0
. We restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{T}}(\mathsf{E}_{1}(s)\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}(s))\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}\cap\mathsf{E}_{4}
in what follows, which for
\mathfrak{d}<\min\{c_{1}/2,c_{2}/2\}
satisfies
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]\leq c_{5}^{-1}e^{-c_{5}(\log N)^{2}}
for some
c_{5}>0
, by a union bound.

By
Lemma

5.2
(and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
), we have for any
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center
\varphi_{0}
for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(0)
that
|\varphi-\varphi_{0}|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
and
|\tilde{\varphi}-\varphi_{0}|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
. Therefore,
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq 2T(\log N)^{2}
, so (
2.9
) implies that

(7.2)

\displaystyle N_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{T}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{3}\leq\tilde{\varphi}\leq N_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{T}{2}\cdot(\log N)^{3}.

Now, fix
t\in[0,T]
, and let
s\in\mathcal{T}
satisfy
|s-t|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{d}(\log N)^{2}}
, so
|s-t^{\prime}|\leq 2e^{-\mathfrak{d}(\log N)^{2}}
. We will first apply
Lemma

3.7
, with the
(\bm{A};\bm{B})
there equal to
(\bm{L}(t);\bm{L}(s))
here, to show that
\varphi
and
\tilde{\varphi}
are localization centers for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(s)
. Then, we will apply our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}(s)
to deduce that
\varphi
and
\tilde{\varphi}
are close to a localization center of
\mu(s)
with respect to
\bm{M}(s)
, and thus are close to each other.

To implement this, we require the third estimate in (
3.5
). So, for any
r\in[0,T]
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote the resolvent
G(z;r)=[G_{ij}(z;r)]=(\bm{L}(r)-z)^{-1}\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}|L_{ij}(s)-L_{ij}(t)|\leq 2e^{-\mathfrak{d}(\log N)^{2}}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}|L_{ij}^{\prime}(t)|\leq e^{-7\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}},

where in the second inequality we used (
2.1
), (
2.4
),
Definition

2.1
, and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
(which together imply that
|L_{ij}^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2(\log N)^{2}
). With (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), this implies that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Omega}\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}|G_{ij}(z;t)-G_{ij}(z;s)|\leq N^{2}\cdot e^{4\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}\cdot e^{-7\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}\leq e^{-2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}},

where we have denoted
\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:e^{-2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
.

Now we apply
Lemma

3.7
, with the parameters
(\bm{A},\bm{B};\lambda;\varphi;\eta,\delta)
there equal to the parameters
(\bm{L}(t),\bm{L}(s);\lambda;\varphi;e^{-2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}},e^{-2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}})
here. This yields an eigenvalue
\lambda^{\prime}\in\eig\bm{L}(s)=\eig\bm{L}(t)
such that
|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|\leq 3N^{2}\zeta^{-1}e^{-2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}<e^{-\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}
and
\varphi
is a
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda^{\prime}
with respect to
\bm{L}(s)
. Due to our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}\subseteq\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(s)}(e^{-\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}})
, we have that
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
, meaning that
\varphi
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(s)
. By similar reasoning,
\tilde{\varphi}
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(s)
.

By our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}(s)
, and the fact from (
2.9
) and (
7.2
) that
\varphi,\tilde{\varphi}\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+(\log N)^{3},N_{2}^{\prime}-(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
, it follows that for any
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center
\psi_{s}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
of
\mu(s)
with respect to
\bm{M}(s)
we have
|\varphi-\psi_{s}|\leq(\log N)^{2}
and
|\tilde{\varphi}-\psi_{s}|\leq(\log N)^{2}
. Hence,
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq 2(\log N)^{2}
, confirming the proposition.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.9
.

This follows from the
\tilde{t}=t
case of
Proposition

7.1
.
∎

7.2.
Spacing Bounds for the Toda Particles

In this section we prove the following lemma approximating the distances between the Toda particles
q_{j}(s)
under thermal equilibrium. Recall we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Lemma 7.2
.

The following two statements hold with overwhelming probability.

(1)

For any
s\in[0,T]
and
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
, we have

(7.3)

\displaystyle\big|q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)-\alpha(i-j)\big|\leq|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}.

(2)

For any
s\in[0,T]
and
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|i-j|\geq T(\log N)^{5}
, we have

(7.4)

\displaystyle\big(q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)\big)\cdot\sgn(\alpha i-\alpha j)\geq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot|i-j|.

Proof.

Observe that it suffices to show for any fixed
s\in[-T,T]
that the below two statements hold with overwhelming probability. First, for any
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
,

(7.5)

\displaystyle|q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)-\alpha(i-j)|\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}.

Second, for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|i-j|\geq T(\log N)^{5}
,

(7.6)

\displaystyle\big(q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)\big)\cdot\sgn(\alpha i-\alpha j)\geq\displaystyle\frac{3|\alpha|}{4}\cdot|i-j|.

Indeed, if this were true then applying a union bound, we may restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
on which (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) hold for all
s\in\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{T}\subset[-T,T]
is an
N^{-20}
-mesh of
[-T,T]
. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, we may further by
Lemma

3.15
restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(s)}(\log N)
. In view of (
2.1
), (
2.3
),
Definition

2.1
, and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, we have
|q_{k}^{\prime}(s)|\leq\log N
for all
s\geq 0
and
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Then, the two statements of the lemma hold for any
s\in[-T,T]
. Indeed, letting
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
be such that
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq N^{-20}
, we have from (
7.5
) and the above bound on
|q_{k}^{\prime}(s)|
that

\displaystyle|q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)-\alpha(i-j)|

\displaystyle\leq|q_{i}(s^{\prime})-q_{j}(s^{\prime})-\alpha(i-j)|+2N^{-20}\log N

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}+2N^{-20}\log N\leq|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2},

which confirms (
7.3
). The verification of (
7.4
) from (
7.6
) is entirely analogous and thus omitted.

Hence, it remains to show (
7.5
) and (
7.6
). Throughout the remainder of this proof, we set
K=T(\log N)^{3}
. By
Proposition

4.4
, there is a family of random variables
\tilde{\bm{a}}=(\tilde{a}_{N_{1}},\tilde{a}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{a}_{N_{2}-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
and
\tilde{\bm{b}}=(\tilde{b}_{N_{1}},\tilde{b}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, such that
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
has the same law as
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
and the following holds. There exists an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which

(7.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|a_{i}(s)-\tilde{a}_{i}|+\displaystyle\max_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|b_{i}(s)-\tilde{b}_{i}|\leq 2e^{-(\log N)^{3}/5}.

We restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows and let
(\tilde{\bm{p}};\tilde{\bm{q}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
denote the Toda state space initial data associated with
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
, as described in
Section

2.1.1
. Further define event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{2}=\bigcap_{i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}\Big\{|q_{i}(0)-q_{i^{\prime}}(0)-\alpha(i-i^{\prime})|+|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}-\alpha(i-i^{\prime})|\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\cdot|i-i^{\prime}|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\Big\},

observing that
\mathsf{E}_{2}
is overwhelmingly probable, by
Lemma

3.12
. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, we also define the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{3}=\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)\cap\bigcap_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\{a_{i}(0)\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\}\cap\{\tilde{a}_{i}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\},

which is overwhelmingly probable, by
Lemma

3.15
and the explicit form of the density
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N-1,N}
for
\bm{a}(0)
(from
Definition

2.4
). Denoting the event
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}
, we restrict to
\mathsf{E}
in what follows; it then suffices to show that (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) hold.

To that end, first observe that, for any
i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle|q_{i}(s)-q_{i^{\prime}}(s)-(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{i^{\prime}})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=i}^{i^{\prime}-1}|\log a_{i}(s)-\log\tilde{a}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq 2N\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket i,i^{\prime}\rrbracket}|a_{i}(s)-\tilde{a}_{i}|\cdot(|a_{i}(s)|^{-1}+|\tilde{a}_{i}|^{-1})\leq N^{-1},

where in the first bound we used (
2.5
), and in the second and third we used (
7.7
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}
. This, together with our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, establishes (
7.5
) and thus the first statement of the lemma. To show the second, we suppose that
\alpha>0
and
i\geq j+T(\log N)^{5}
, as the proof when
\alpha<0
or
i\leq j-T(\log N)^{5}
is entirely analogous. Then, observe

\displaystyle q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)

\displaystyle\geq q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)-|q_{i}(s)-q_{i}(0)|-|q_{j}(s)-q_{j}(0)|

\displaystyle\geq\alpha(i-j)-|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}-2s\cdot\displaystyle\max_{|s^{\prime}|\leq s}|b_{i}(s)|

\displaystyle\geq\displaystyle\frac{4\alpha}{5}\cdot(i-j)-2T\log N\geq\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot(i-j),

where in the first and second statements we used our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}
, the first equality in (
2.1
), and the fact that
p_{i}(t)=b_{i}(t)
by (
2.3
); in the third we used the fact that
i-j\geq T(\log N)^{5}
and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
; and in the fourth we again used the fact that
i-j\geq T(\log N)^{5}
. This confirms (
7.6
) and thus the second statement of the lemma when
i\geq j+T(\log N)^{5}
; since (as mentioned above) the proof when
i\leq j-T(\log N)^{5}
is entirely analogous, this establishes the lemma.
∎

7.3.
Proof of
Proposition

2.10

In this section we establish
Proposition

2.10
. We begin with the following lemma proving a variant of (
2.11
), when the second sum appearing there is over
i
such that
\varphi_{t}(i)
(as opposed to
q_{i}(t)
or
Q_{i}(t)
) is in a prescribed interval. Recall we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Proposition 7.3
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Let
N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
be indices satisfying

(7.8)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+N^{1/100}\leq N_{1}^{\prime}\leq N_{2}^{\prime}\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}-N^{1/100}.

Then, the following two statements hold for any real number
t\in[0,T]
and integer
m\in\llbracket 0,\log N\rrbracket
.

(1)

We have

(7.9)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\lambda_{i}^{m}\Bigg|\leq 12(\log N)^{m+3}.

(2)

For any
k\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, we have

(7.10)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i<k}\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)\leq k}\lambda_{i}^{m}\Bigg|\leq 12(\log N)^{m+3}.

Proof.

The proofs of (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) are very similar, so we only focus on the former. We may assume in what follows that
(N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime})
is fixed, by a union bound. Let us first establish (
7.9
) for any deterministic
t\in[0,T]
, and then we will show it holds for all
t\in[0,T]
.

So, fix
t\in[0,T]
. We first apply
Proposition

4.4
to deduce the existence of a random matrix
\bm{P}=[P_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
with the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
, and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which we have

(7.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}-m,N_{2}^{\prime}+m\rrbracket}|P_{ij}-L_{ij}(t)|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}}.

where we used the fact that
\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}-m,N_{2}^{\prime}+m\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{4},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{4}\rrbracket
. Further set
N^{\prime}=N_{2}^{\prime}-N_{1}^{\prime}+1
and define the
N^{\prime}\times N^{\prime}
symmetric matrix
\bm{Q}=[Q_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}
by setting
Q_{ij}=P_{ij}
whenever
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
. Set
\eig\bm{Q}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{N^{\prime}})
, and let
\psi:\llbracket 1,N^{\prime}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
denote a
\zeta
-localization center bijection for
\bm{Q}
. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, we define the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(t)}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{P}}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{Q}}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
, which by
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

3.18
is overwhelmingly probable. We restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}
in the below.

We will proceed by comparing the first sum on the left side of (
7.9
) to
\Tr\bm{Q}=\sum_{\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}}\mu^{m}
; use
Corollary

5.6
to approximate the eigenvalues of
\bm{Q}
by those of
\bm{L}(t)
; and compare the resulting expression to the second sum on the left side of (
7.9
). To implement the first task, observe that

(7.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}\Bigg|&=\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}[\bm{L}(t)^{m}]_{ii}-\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}\Bigg|\\
&\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}[\bm{P}^{m}]_{ii}-\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}\Bigg|+Ne^{-(\log N)^{3}}m(\log N)^{m},\end{aligned}

where we have denoted the
(i,j)
entry of any matrix
\bm{M}
by
[\bm{M}]_{ij}
; here, the first statement holds by
Definition

2.3
and the second by (
7.11
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
. Next, since
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
are tridiagonal and satisfy
P_{ij}=Q_{ij}
whenever
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, observe that
[\bm{P}^{m}]_{ii}=[\bm{Q}^{m}]_{ii}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+m,N_{2}^{\prime}-m\rrbracket
. We further have by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
that each entry of
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
is bounded by
\log N
, meaning (again since
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
are tridiagonal) that each entry of
\bm{P}^{m}
and
\bm{Q}^{m}
is bounded by
(3\log N)^{m}
. It follows that

(7.13)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}[\bm{P}^{m}]_{ii}-\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}\Bigg|\leq\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket\\
i\notin\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime}+m,N_{2}^{\prime}-m\rrbracket\end{subarray}}\big(|(\bm{P}^{m})_{ii}|+|(\bm{Q}^{m})_{ii}|\big)\leq 4m(3\log N)^{m}.

Next, denote
N_{1}^{\prime\prime}=N_{1}^{\prime}+(\log N)^{3}
and
N_{2}^{\prime\prime}=N_{2}^{\prime}-(\log N)^{3}
. Then,

(7.14)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}-\displaystyle\sum_{j:\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket}\mu_{j}^{m}\Bigg|\leq 2(\log N)^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}}|\mu|^{m}\leq 2(\log N)^{m+3},

where in the first bound we used that
\Tr\bm{Q}^{m}=\sum_{\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}}\mu^{m}
and
|\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket|\leq 2(\log N)^{3}
, and in the second we used the fact that
|\mu|\leq\log N
for any
\mu\in\eig\bm{Q}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
).

Now, we apply
Proposition

7.1
and
Corollary

5.5
, the latter with the
(\bm{L};\tilde{\bm{L}};\delta;\mathcal{D})
there equal to
(\bm{Q};\bm{L}(t);e^{-(\log N)^{3}};\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket)
here. This by (
7.11
) yields a constant
c>0
, such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. There exists a function
\kappa:\llbracket 1,N^{\prime}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
such that, for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N^{\prime}\rrbracket
with
\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
,

(7.15)

\displaystyle|\mu_{j}-\lambda_{\kappa(j)}|\leq e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{3}},\quad\text{and}\quad\big|\psi(j)-\varphi_{t}(\kappa(j))\big|\leq(\log N)^{3}.

The second statement in (
7.15
) holds since
Corollary

5.5
implies that
\psi(j)
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center of
\lambda_{\kappa(j)}
with respect to
\bm{L}(t)
; therefore, since
\varphi_{t}(\kappa(j))
is as well,
Proposition

7.1
implies (as
\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
) that
|\psi(j)-\varphi_{t}(\kappa(j))|\leq(\log N)^{3}
. Further observe that, due our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, we have
|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i^{\prime}}|\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\geq 2e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{3}}
for any distinct
i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
; thus,
\kappa(j)\neq\kappa(j^{\prime})
for any distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,N^{\prime}\rrbracket
with
\psi(j),\psi(j^{\prime})\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
. Therefore,

(7.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&\displaystyle\sum_{j:\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket}\mu_{j}^{m}-\displaystyle\sum_{j:\varphi_{t}(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\lambda_{j}^{m}\Bigg|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{j:\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket}|\mu_{j}^{m}-\lambda_{\kappa(j)}^{m}|+2(\log N)^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t)}|\lambda|^{m}\leq 4(\log N)^{m+3},\end{aligned}

where the first statement holds by changing variables from
j
to
\kappa(j)
in the second sum there whenever
\psi(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
, and using (
7.15
) with the facts that
\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime}-(\log N)^{3},N_{2}+(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket=\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and that
|\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket|\leq 2(\log N)^{3}
; the second follows from (
7.15
) with the fact that
|\lambda|\leq\log N
for each
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
(as we restricted to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
). Summing (
7.12
), (
7.13
), (
7.14
), and (
7.16
), we deduce that

(7.17)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\lambda_{i}^{m}\Bigg|\leq 7(\log N)^{m+3}.

This verifies that (
7.9
) holds with overwhelming probability for a fixed
t\in[0,T]
; it remains to show it holds with high probability for all
t\in[0,T]
simultaneously. To that end, for any
t\in[0,T]
, let
\mathsf{E}(t)
denote the event on which (
7.17
) holds. We then have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}(t)^{\complement}]\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{2}}
for some constant
c_{2}>0
. Denoting
\mathfrak{c}=c_{2}/2
, it follows from
Proposition

7.1
that there exists a constant
c_{3}>0
and an event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{4}^{\complement}]\leq c_{3}^{-1}e^{-c_{3}(\log N)^{2}}
such that the following holds on
\mathsf{E}_{4}
. For any
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,T]
with
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
, we have
|\varphi_{s}(i)-\varphi_{s^{\prime}}(i)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
, whenenever
\varphi=\varphi_{s}(i)
satisfies (
2.9
). Letting
\mathcal{T}
denote an
e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
-mesh of
[0,T]
, we restrict to the event
\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\mathsf{E}(s)\cap\mathsf{E}_{4}\cap\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)
, which we may by a union bound (and
Lemma

3.15
).

Now let
t\in[0,T]
be arbitrary, and let
s\in\mathcal{T}
satisfy
|t-s|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
. Then, we claim that

(7.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{2}^{\prime}}\big|\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(s)\big|\Bigg|\leq 1;\\
&\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\lambda_{i}^{m}-\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{s}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\lambda_{i}^{m}\Bigg|\leq 4(\log N)^{m+3}.\end{aligned}

which together with (
7.17
) (with the
t
there equal to
s
here) would imply the proposition.

To verify the first bound in (
7.18
), observe for any
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle|L_{ij}(t)-L_{ij}(s)|\leq|s-t|\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[s,t]}|L_{ij}^{\prime}(r)|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}\cdot 2(\log N)^{2}\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}/2},

where in the second inequality we used
Definition

2.1
, (
2.3
), (
2.4
), and our restriction to the event
\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)
. Since the same event implies that each entry of
\bm{L}(s)
and
\bm{L}(t)
is bounded by
\log N
, and both of these matrices are tridiagonal, it follows for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\big|\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)-\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(s)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|[\bm{L}(t)^{m}]_{ii}-[\bm{L}(s)^{m}]_{ii}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}|L_{ij}(s)-L_{ij}(t)|\cdot m(3\log N)^{m}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}/2}\cdot m(3\log N)^{m}\leq N^{-1}.

Summing over
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
confirms the first bound in (
7.18
). The second bound in (
7.18
) follows from the fact that
|\lambda|\leq\log N
for any
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(t)}(\log N)
), with the fact that there are at most
4(\log N)^{3}
indices
i
for which
\varphi_{s}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and
\varphi_{t}(i)\notin\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
or for which
\varphi_{s}(i)\notin\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and
\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
(as
|\varphi_{t}(i)-\varphi_{s}(i)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
). This proves (
7.18
) and thus (
7.9
).

As mentioned previously, the proof of (
7.10
) is entirely analogous, and is obtained by replacing the application of
Proposition

4.4
and
Corollary

5.6
above with
Proposition

6.3
(with the
\ell
there equal to
k
here); we omit further details.
∎

We can now prove
Proposition

2.10
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.10
.

We assume that
\alpha>0
in what follows, as the proof when
\alpha<0
is entirely analogous. The proofs of (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) are very similar, so we only focus on the former. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, define the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\bigcap_{s\in[0,t]}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(s)}(\log N)
. By
Lemma

3.15
,
\mathsf{E}_{1}
holds with overwhelming probability, so we restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows. We further restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
on which
Lemma

7.2
holds, with the
T
there both equal to
0
and
T
here (we may take the
T
there to be
0
by the
R=(\log N)^{2}|i-j|^{1/2}
case of
Lemma

3.12
).

Fix
t\in[0,T]
, and let
N_{1}^{\prime}=\min\{i:q_{i}(t)\in\mathcal{J}\}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}=\max\{i:q_{i}(t)\in\mathcal{J}\}
. We claim that
(N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime})
satisfy (
7.8
). To verify this, assume to the contrary, so that either
N_{1}^{\prime}<N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+N^{1/100}
or
N_{2}^{\prime}>N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}-N^{1/100}
holds. The analysis of these two cases is entirely analogous, so we assume the former. Then,

\displaystyle q_{N_{1}^{\prime}}(0)-q_{0}(0)\geq q_{N_{1}^{\prime}}(t)-T\log N

\displaystyle\geq\alpha N_{1}+(T+|N_{1}|^{1/2})(\log N)^{5}-T\log N

\displaystyle\geq\alpha N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot|N_{1}|^{1/2}(\log N)^{5},

where the first statement holds since
q_{0}(0)=0
and
|q_{i}^{\prime}(t)|=|p_{i}(t)|\leq\log N
(by (
2.1
), (
2.3
),
Definition

2.1
, and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
); the second holds by (
2.10
) and the inclusion
q_{N_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\in\mathcal{J}
; and the third holds for
N
sufficiently large, by (
2.7
). This contradicts (
7.3
) (at
s=T=0
), confirming (
7.8
).

Therefore,
Proposition

7.3
applies and implies that (
7.9
) holds with overwhelming probability. Then, since
q_{i}(t)\in\mathcal{J}
implies that
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, it suffices (by (
2.8
)) to show that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket:q_{i}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}}|\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)|+\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket,q_{\varphi_{t}(i)}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}}|\lambda_{i}|^{m}\leq(3\log N)^{m+5}.

Changing variables in the second sum from
i
to
\varphi_{t}^{-1}(i)
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket:q_{i}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}}|\mathfrak{k}_{i}^{[m]}(t)|+\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket:q_{i}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}}|\lambda_{\varphi_{t}^{-1}(i)}|^{m}\leq(3\log N)^{m+5}.

Using the facts that each entry of
\bm{L}(t)^{m}
is bounded by
(3\log N)^{m}
(as
\bm{L}(t)
is tridiagonal and each of its entries is bounded by
\log N
) and that
|\lambda|\leq\log N
for each
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
, it remains to verify

(7.19)

\displaystyle\#\{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket:q_{i}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}\}\leq 2(\log N)^{9/2}.

To show this, observe that if
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
satisfies
q_{i}(t)\notin\mathcal{J}
, then either
q_{i}(t)<q_{N_{1}^{\prime}}(t)
or
q_{i}(t)>q_{N_{2}^{\prime}}(t)
. In the former case, we have

\displaystyle 0>q_{i}(t)-q_{N_{1}^{\prime}}(t)\geq\alpha\cdot(i-N_{1}^{\prime})-|i-N_{1}^{\prime}|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2},

where the last inequality holds by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
. Hence
i<N_{1}^{\prime}+(\log N)^{9/2}
, and there are at most
(\log N)^{9/2}
such indices in
\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
. By similar reasoning, there are at most
(\log N)^{9/2}
indices
i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
such that
q_{i}(t)>q_{N_{2}^{\prime}}(t)
. Summing these bounds yields (
7.19
) and thus (
2.11
).

As mentioned previously, the proof of (
2.12
) is entirely analogous (using (
7.10
) in place of (
7.9
)).
∎

8.
Proof of the Asymptotic Scattering Relation

8.1.
Proof of
Theorem

2.11

In this section we prove
Theorem

2.11
, which will follow from the following variant of it that replaces the
Q_{t}(i)
-dependent sums in (
2.14
) with ones that depend on
\varphi_{t}(i)
. The latter will be shown in
Section

8.3
below. We adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Theorem 8.1
.

The following holds with overwhelming probability. Let
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfy (
2.13
). Then, for any
t\in[0,T]
, we have

(8.1)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\lambda_{k}t-Q_{k}(t)+Q_{k}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq 2(\log N)^{12}.

Proof of
Theorem

2.11
.

Throughout this proof, we assume that
\alpha>0
, as the proof when
\alpha<0
is entirely analogous; we also fix
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
2.13
). We begin by restricting to several events. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, first restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(0)}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
, which we may by
Lemma

3.15
,
Lemma

3.18
, and a union bound. Further restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
on which
Theorem

8.1
holds; to the event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
on which
Lemma

7.2
holds; and to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
on which
Lemma

5.2
holds.

In view of (
8.1
) (and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
), we must show that

\displaystyle 2\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big|(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)})-(

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(t)<Q_{k}(t)}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)})\big|\cdot\big|\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\big|

\displaystyle\leq(\log N)^{15}-2(\log N)^{12}.

Since by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
we that
|\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}||\leq(\log N)^{2}
for each
i\neq k
, it therefore suffices to show that

(8.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big|(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)})-(\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(t)<Q_{k}(t)}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)})\big|\leq(\log N)^{8}.

We first claim that the summand on the left side of (
8.2
) is equal to
0
if
i
satisfies
|\varphi_{0}(i)-\varphi_{0}(k)|>2T(\log N)^{5}
. Indeed, fix such an index
i
, and assume
\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)-2T(\log N)^{5}
, as the verification in the alternative case is entirely analogous. Then, (
5.1
) (with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
) gives
|\varphi_{t}(i)-\varphi_{0}(i)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
and
|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
, so for sufficiently large
N
we have
\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)-T(\log N)^{5}
. As such,
\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}=1=\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}
. By (
7.4
) (and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
) whose assumption holds by the above bounds, we have for each
s\in\{0,t\}
that

\displaystyle Q_{k}(s)-Q_{i}(s)=q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}-q_{\varphi_{s}(i)}\geq\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot\big(\varphi_{s}(k)-\varphi_{s}(i)\big)>0.

So, it follows that
\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(t)<Q_{k}(t)}=1=\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)}
, meaning that the summand on the left side of (
8.2
) associated with
i
is equal to
0
.

Therefore, we may restrict the sum on the left side of (
8.2
) to indices
i
satisfying
|\varphi_{0}(i)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq 2T(\log N)^{5}
. Fix such an index
i
; we claim for each
s\in\{0,t\}
that

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}=\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(s)<Q_{k}(s)},\qquad\text{unless $|\varphi_{s}(i)-\varphi_{s}(k)|\leq(\log N)^{5}$},

which would imply (
8.2
) and thus the theorem. To confirm (
8.3
), fix
s\in\{0,t\}
, and assume that
|\varphi_{s}(i)-\varphi_{s}(k)|>(\log N)^{5}
; we will further suppose that
\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)-(\log N)^{5}
, as the proof in the alternative case is entirely analogous. By our assumptions (
2.13
) and
|\varphi_{0}(i)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq 2T(\log N)^{5}
, observe (by (
5.1
), using our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
) that
\varphi_{s}(i),\varphi_{s}(k)\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}\rrbracket
, so (
7.3
) holds (by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
) with the
(i,j)
there equal to
(\varphi_{s}(i),\varphi_{s}(k))
here. Hence,

\displaystyle Q_{k}(s)-Q_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=q_{\varphi_{k}(s)}(s)-q_{\varphi_{i}(s)}(s)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\cdot\big(\varphi_{k}(s)-\varphi_{i}(s)\big)-|\varphi_{k}(s)-\varphi_{i}(s)|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2}>0,

where the last bound holds since
\varphi_{k}(s)-\varphi_{i}(s)\geq(\log N)^{5}
. Therefore,
\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(s)<Q_{k}(s)}=1=\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}
, proving (
8.3
) and thus the theorem.
∎

8.2.
Proof of
Theorem

8.1
if
T^{2}\leq N

In this section we establish the following case of
Theorem

8.1
, which assumes that
T^{2}\leq N
(and a slightly stronger condition (
8.4
) on
\varphi_{0}(k)
than imposed in (
2.13
)) and addresses a fixed time
t\in[0,T]
. Recall we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Proposition 8.2
.

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the following holds with overwhelming probability. Let
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfy

(8.4)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{6}+N^{1/100}\leq\varphi_{0}(k)\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{6}-N^{1/100}.

If
T^{2}\leq N
, then we have

(8.5)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\lambda_{k}t-Q_{k}(t)+Q_{k}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq 2(\log N)^{11}.

Proposition
8.2
is a quick consequence of the below two lemmas. In what follows, we denote

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(s)=\log\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-\lambda_{j}s}u_{j}(N_{1};0)^{2}.

Lemma 8.3
.

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
8.4
), we have

(8.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|Q_{k}(t)-Q_{k}(0)+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|&\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|-\lambda_{k}t\\
&-\mathfrak{C}(t)-q_{N_{1}}(t)+q_{N_{1}}(0)\Bigg|\leq 2(\log N)^{6}.\end{aligned}

Lemma 8.4
.

Fix
t\in[0,T]
, and assume that
T^{2}\leq N
. There exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[|q_{N_{1}}(0)-q_{N_{1}}(t)-\mathfrak{C}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{11}\big]\geq 1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}.

Proof of
Proposition

8.2
.

This follows from
Lemma

8.3
and
Lemma

8.4
.
∎

We now show
Lemma

8.3
and
Lemma

8.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.3
.

Fix
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
8.4
), and observe by
Lemma

3.6
that

(8.8)

\displaystyle-2\log|u_{k}(N_{1};t)|=\lambda_{k}t-2\log|u_{k}(N_{1};0)|+\mathfrak{C}(t).

By
Lemma

5.2
, there is an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which we have
|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
. Observe on
\mathsf{E}_{1}
that
N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+N^{1/100}\leq\varphi_{s}(k)\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}-N^{1/100}
for each
s\in\{0,t\}
, by (
8.4
). We restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows.

By
Proposition

6.1
, there exists an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, on which the following holds. For any real number
s\in\{0,t\}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|2\log|u_{k}(N_{1};s)|-2\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{s}(k)-1}\log L_{i,i+1}(s)+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\Bigg|\leq 2(\log N)^{6},

where we used that
N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5}+N^{1/100}\leq\varphi_{s}(k)\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}-N^{1/100}
. We further restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
below. Since
Definition

2.1
, (
2.3
), and
Assumption

2.8
together imply that

\displaystyle 2\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{s}(k)-1}\log L_{i,i+1}(s)=2\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{\varphi_{s}(k)-1}\log a_{i}(s)=q_{N_{1}}(s)-q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(s)=q_{N_{1}}(s)-Q_{k}(s),

we obtain by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}
that, for each
s\in\{0,t\}
,

\displaystyle\Bigg|2\log|u_{k}(N_{1};s)|+Q_{k}(s)-q_{N_{1}}(s)+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\Bigg|\leq(\log N)^{6}.

Subtracting this bound at
s=0
from it at
s=t
, and applying (
8.8
), then yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

8.4
.

We will establish this proposition by averaging an estimate on the left side of (
8.7
) over all
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. To that end, let
\mathsf{E}_{1}
denote the event on which (
8.7
) holds for each
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
satisfying (
8.4
). By
Lemma

8.3
,
\mathsf{E}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable. We will define additional events on which we can bound the left side of (
8.7
) for
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
not necessarily satisfying (
8.4
). Specifically, recalling
Definition

3.13
, set

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{2}=\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}});

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{3}=\bigcap_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big\{|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}\big\}.

Then
\mathsf{E}_{2}
is overwhelmingly probable, by
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

3.18
, and
\mathsf{E}_{3}
is overwhelmingly probable, by
Lemma

5.2
. We further define the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{4}=\bigcap_{|i-j|\leq T(\log N)^{2}}\{|q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)|\leq T(\log N)^{3}\},

which by
Lemma

3.12
and a union bound is overwhelmingly probable. Defining the event
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}\cap\mathsf{E}_{4}
, we may by a union bound restrict to
\mathsf{E}
in what follows.

Then, for any
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, we have

(8.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&Q_{k}(t)-Q_{k}(0)-q_{N_{1}}(t)+q_{N_{1}}(0)-\lambda_{k}t-\mathfrak{C}(t)\\
&+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\Bigg|\\
&\quad\leq|q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(0)-q_{\varphi_{0}(k)}(0)|+|q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(t)-q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(0)|+|q_{N_{1}}(t)-q_{N_{1}}(0)|\\
&\qquad+T\log N+|\mathfrak{C}(t)|+2(\log N)^{2}\cdot\#\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k),\varphi_{0}(i)>\varphi_{0}(k)\}\\
&\qquad+2(\log N)^{2}\cdot\#\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:\varphi_{t}(i)>\varphi_{t}(k),\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)\},\end{aligned}

where we used the definition
Q_{k}(s)=q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(s)
(from (
2.8
)) and the facts that
|\lambda_{k}|\leq\log N
and that
|\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}||\leq(\log N)^{2}
(both by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
). We next bound the terms on the right side of (
8.9
). Observe by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
that

(8.10)

\displaystyle|q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(0)-q_{\varphi_{0}(k)}(0)|\leq T(\log N)^{3},

since
|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{0}(k)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
. Additionally,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}|q_{j}(t)-q_{j}(0)|\leq\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|b_{j}(s)|ds\leq T\log N,

where in the first inequality we used (
2.1
) and (
2.3
); in the second we used the fact that
t\in[0,T]
and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
. Moreover,

(8.12)

\displaystyle-2T\log N\leq-t\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|\lambda|-\log N\leq\mathfrak{C}(t)\leq t\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|\lambda|+\log N\leq 2T\log N,

where the first and fourth inequalities follow from our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
; the second from the definition (
8.6
) of
\mathfrak{C}(t)
, with the fact that there exists at least one index
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
for which
u_{j}(N_{1};0)^{2}\geq N^{-1}
; and the third from the definition (
8.6
) of
\mathfrak{C}(t)
, with the fact that
u_{j}(N_{1};0)^{2}\leq 1
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. We further have that

(8.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\#\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k),\varphi_{0}(i)>\varphi_{0}(k)\}\leq 2T(\log N)^{2};\\
&\#\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:\varphi_{t}(i)>\varphi_{t}(k),\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)\}\leq 2T(\log N)^{2}.\end{aligned}

Indeed, to verify the first bound in (
8.13
), observe (by our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
) that if
\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)-2T(\log N)^{2}
then
\varphi_{0}(i)\leq\varphi_{t}(i)+T(\log N)^{2}<\varphi_{t}(k)-T(\log N)^{2}\leq\varphi_{0}(k)
. In particular, we can only have
\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)
and
\varphi_{0}(i)>\varphi_{0}(k)
if
\varphi_{t}(i)\in\llbracket\varphi_{t}(k)-2T(\log N)^{2},\varphi_{t}(k)-1\rrbracket
, meaning that there are at most
2T(\log N)^{2}
such indices
i
. This shows the first statement in (
8.13
); the second is confirmed entirely analogously.

Inserting (
8.10
), (
8.11
), (
8.12
), and (
8.13
) into (
8.9
), we obtain for any
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|Q_{k}

\displaystyle(t)-Q_{k}(0)-q_{N_{1}}(t)+q_{N_{1}}(0)-\lambda_{k}t-\mathfrak{C}(t)

\displaystyle+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq T(\log N)^{3}+5T\log N+8T(\log N)^{4}\leq 9T(\log N)^{4}.

Summing this inequality over
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
\varphi_{0}(k)\notin\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{6}+N^{1/100},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{6}-N^{1/100}\rrbracket
with (
8.7
) over
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
\varphi_{0}\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{6}+N^{1/100},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{6}-N^{1/100}\rrbracket
, we obtain

(8.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}(q_{k}(t)-q_{k}(0))-t\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}+2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|\\
&\quad-2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}|+N\cdot\big(q_{N_{1}}(0)-q_{N_{1}}(t)-\mathfrak{C}(t)\big)\Bigg|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq 2N(\log N)^{6}+18T^{2}(\log N)^{10}+18TN^{1/100}(\log N)^{4}.\end{aligned}

The difference between of the third and fourth terms on the left side of (
8.14
) is equal to
0
, since both are equal to
\sum_{i\neq j}\log|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|
. The difference between the first and the second is also equal to
0
, as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}(q_{k}(t)-q_{k}(0))=\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}p_{k}(s)ds=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Tr\bm{L}(s)ds=t\cdot\Tr\bm{L}(0)=t\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j},

where the first statement follows from (
2.1
); the second from (
2.3
) and
Definition

2.1
; and the third and fourth from
Lemma

2.2
. It follows from (
8.14
) that

\displaystyle\big|q_{N_{1}}

\displaystyle(0)-q_{N_{1}}(t)-\mathfrak{C}(t)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2(\log N)^{6}+18T^{2}N^{-1}(\log N)^{10}+18TN^{-99/100}(\log N)^{4}\leq(\log N)^{11},

where we used the fact that
T^{2}\leq N
. This establishes the lemma.
∎

8.3.
Proof of
Theorem

8.1

In this section we establish
Theorem

8.1
. We first prove the following variant of it that fixes the time
t\in[0,T]
. Recall we adopt
Assumption

2.8
throughout.

Theorem 8.5
.

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
2.13
), we have

(8.15)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\lambda_{k}t-Q_{k}(t)+Q_{k}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq(\log N)^{12}.

Proof.

By
Proposition

8.2
, (
8.5
) holds with overwhelming probability, if
T^{2}\leq N
. It suffices to show such a bound continues to hold for larger values of
T\leq N(\log N)^{-7}
(and to weaken the constraint (
8.4
) on
k
to (
2.13
)).

To that end, we first apply (
8.5
) on a Toda lattice on a larger interval (at thermal equilibrium), and then use comparison estimates (such as
Corollary

5.6
and
Lemma

4.5
) to approximate the original Toda lattice by the enlarged one. To implement this, first let
\tilde{N}_{1}\leq\tilde{N}_{2}
be integers satisfying

(8.16)

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{1}+T^{2}N\leq N_{1}\leq N_{2}\leq\tilde{N}_{2}-T^{2}N,\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{N}\leq N^{5},

where
\tilde{N}=\tilde{N}_{2}-\tilde{N}_{1}+1
. Let
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(s);\tilde{\bm{b}}(s))\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{N}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{N}}
denote the Flaschka variables for a Toda lattice on
\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket
; letting
\tilde{\bm{a}}(s)=(\tilde{a}_{\tilde{N}_{1}}(s),\tilde{a}_{\tilde{N}_{1}+1}(s),\ldots,\tilde{a}_{\tilde{N}_{2}}(s))
and
\tilde{\bm{b}}(s)=(\tilde{b}_{\tilde{N}_{1}}(s),\tilde{b}_{\tilde{N}_{1}+1}(s),\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\tilde{N}_{2}}(s))
, they satisfy
\tilde{a}_{\tilde{N}_{2}}(s)=0
, and (
2.4
) holds for each
(j,t)\in\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{R}
. We sample the initial data
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(0);\tilde{\bm{b}}(0))
according to the thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;\tilde{N}-1,\tilde{N}}
of
Definition

2.4
; we couple
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(0);\tilde{\bm{b}}(0))
with
(\bm{a}(0);\bm{b}(0))
so that
(\tilde{a}_{i}(0),\tilde{b}_{i}(0))=(a_{i}(0),b_{i}(0))
for all
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
.

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, denote the Lax matrix associated with
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(s);\tilde{\bm{b}}(s))
(as in
Definition

2.1
) by
\tilde{\bm{L}}(s)=[\tilde{L}_{ij}(s)]\in\SymMat_{\tilde{N}\times\tilde{N}}
. Set
\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}(s)=(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\tilde{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{N}})
, which does not depend on
s
(by
Lemma

2.2
). For each
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
\tilde{\varphi}_{s}:\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket
denote a
\zeta
-localization center bijection for
\tilde{\bm{L}}(s)
. Further let
(\tilde{\bm{p}}(s);\tilde{\bm{q}}(s))\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{N}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{N}}
denote the Toda state space variables associated with
(\tilde{\bm{a}}(s);\tilde{\bm{b}}(s))
, as in
Section

2.1.1
, where we have indexed
\tilde{\bm{p}}(s)=(\tilde{p}_{\tilde{N}_{1}}(s),\tilde{p}_{\tilde{N}_{1}+1}(s),\ldots,\tilde{p}_{\tilde{N}_{2}}(s))
and
\tilde{\bm{q}}(s)=(\tilde{q}_{\tilde{N}_{1}}(s),\tilde{q}_{\tilde{N}_{1}+1}(s),\ldots,\tilde{q}_{\tilde{N}_{2}}(s))
. For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket
, denote
\tilde{Q}_{i}(s)=\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{s}(i)}(s)
.

By (
8.5
) and the fact that
T^{2}\leq\tilde{N}\leq N^{5}
, there exists an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which we have

(8.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\tilde{\lambda}_{m}t-\tilde{Q}_{m}(t)+\tilde{Q}_{m}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(m)}\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{m}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|+2&\displaystyle\sum_{i:\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(m)}\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{m}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|\Bigg|\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq 5^{12}(\log N)^{11},\end{aligned}

for any
m\in\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket
satisfying

(8.18)

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{1}+T(\log\tilde{N})^{6}+\tilde{N}^{1/100}\leq\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(m)\leq\tilde{N}_{2}-T(\log\tilde{N})^{6}-\tilde{N}^{1/100}.

In what follows, we restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
. We must therefore approximate the parameters
(\tilde{Q}_{j}(s),\tilde{\lambda}_{j},\tilde{\varphi}_{s}(j))
by
(Q_{j}(s),\lambda_{j},\varphi_{s}(j))
, which we will do using
Lemma

4.5
and
Corollary

5.6
(with the fact that
\bm{q}(0)
and
\tilde{\bm{q}}(0)
coincide near the origin).

To do so, we restrict to several additional events. Recalling
Definition

3.13
, restrict to the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\tilde{\bm{L}}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cap\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\log N}{1600}\Big)\cap\mathsf{BND}_{\tilde{\bm{L}}(r)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\log N}{1600}\Big),

which we may by
Lemma

3.15
and
Lemma

3.18
. Further restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
on which
Lemma

7.2
holds, with the
\bm{q}
there equal to both
\bm{q}
here and
\tilde{\bm{q}}
here. Additionally restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
on which
Lemma

5.2
holds, with the
(\bm{L};\varphi_{j})
there equal to both
(\bm{L}(0);\varphi_{0}(j))
and
(\tilde{\bm{L}}(0);\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(j))
here, and on which
Proposition

2.9
holds, with the
\bm{L}(t)
there equal to both
\bm{L}(t)
and
\tilde{\bm{L}}(t)
here.

Now, setting
K=T(\log N)^{3}/2
, we have by (the
A=\log N/400
case of)
Lemma

4.5
that

(8.19)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\displaystyle\max_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}\big(|a_{i}(t)-\tilde{a}_{i}(t)|+|b_{i}(t)-\tilde{b}_{i}(t)|\big)\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}/10}.

Next, by
Proposition

4.4
, there are random matrices
\bm{M}=[M_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
and
\tilde{\bm{M}}=[\tilde{M}_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket}
with the same laws as
\bm{L}(0)
and
\tilde{\bm{L}}(0)
, respectively, and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{5}
, on which we have

(8.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|M_{ij}-L_{ij}(t)|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}/10};\\
&\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|\tilde{M}_{ij}-\tilde{L}_{ij}(t)|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{3}/10}.\end{aligned}

Restricting to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
, we may therefore (by (
8.19
) and (
8.20
)) further restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{6}
on which
Corollary

5.5
and
Corollary

5.6
hold, with the
(\delta;\mathcal{D})
there equal to
(2e^{-(\log N)^{3}/10};\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket)
here, and the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}})
equal to any of
(\bm{L}(0),\tilde{\bm{L}}(0))
,
(\bm{M},\bm{L}(t))
,
(\tilde{\bm{M}},\tilde{\bm{L}}(t))
, and
(\tilde{\bm{M}},\bm{M})
here (where we view
\bm{M}
and
\bm{L}(0)
as
\tilde{N}\times\tilde{N}
matrices by setting
M_{ij}=L_{ij}(0)=0
if
(i,j)\in\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1},\tilde{N}_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
).

Now, let
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
be any index satisfying

(8.21)

\displaystyle N_{1}+3T(\log\tilde{N})^{3}\leq\varphi_{0}(i)\leq N_{2}-3T(\log\tilde{N})^{3}.

Our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
implies from (
5.1
) that
N_{1}+2T(\log\tilde{N})^{3}\leq\varphi_{t}(i)\leq N_{2}-2T(\log\tilde{N})^{3}
. Recalling that
T\geq N^{1/2}\geq 250
(as otherwise the theorem follows from
Proposition

8.2
), this will enable us to use our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{6}
to apply
Corollary

5.6
twice, first with the
(\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}})
there equal to
(\bm{M};\bm{L}(t))
here and then with them equal to
(\tilde{\bm{M}};\bm{M})
, and also to apply
Corollary

5.5
with the
(\bm{L};\tilde{\bm{L}})
there equal to
(\tilde{\bm{M}};\tilde{\bm{L}}(t))
. The first yields a constant
c_{2}>0
and an eigenvalue
\mu\in\eig\bm{M}
such that
|\mu-\lambda_{i}|\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}}
and
\varphi_{t}(i)
is an
N^{-1}\zeta
-localization center for
\mu
with respect to
\bm{M}
. The second yields an eigenvalue
\tilde{\mu}\in\eig\tilde{\bm{M}}
such that
|\mu-\tilde{\mu}|\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}}
and
\varphi_{t}(i)
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center for
\tilde{\mu}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{M}}
. The third yields an index
\sigma(i)\in\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket
such that
|\tilde{\mu}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}|\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}}
and
\varphi_{t}(i)
is an
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center of
\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}(t)
. By
Proposition

2.9
(and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
), we have
|\varphi_{t}(i)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(i))|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{3}
, and hence

(8.22)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\lambda_{i}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}|\leq 3c_{2}^{-1}&e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}};\quad\big|\varphi_{t}(i)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(i))\big|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{3};\\
&\big|\varphi_{0}(i)-\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\sigma(i))\big|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{3},\end{aligned}

where the last bound follows again from similar reasoning to the second (taken at
t=0
). Observe by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{SEP}_{\tilde{\bm{L}}(t)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
and the first inequality in (
8.22
) that the map from
i
to
\sigma(i)
is injective.

Then, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
on which (
8.17
) holds (taking the
m
there to be
\sigma(k)
, which satisfies (
8.18
) by (
2.13
) and (
8.22
)), and the fact that
\llbracket N_{1}+(\log N)^{6},N_{2}-(\log N)^{6}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket N_{1}+3(\log\tilde{N})^{3},N_{2}-3(\log\tilde{N})^{3}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1}+T(\log\tilde{N})^{6}+\tilde{N}^{1/100},\tilde{N}_{2}-T(\log\tilde{N})^{6}-\tilde{N}^{1/100}\rrbracket
(by (
8.16
)), it suffices to show that

(8.23)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad|\lambda_{k}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}|\leq T^{-1};\\
&|Q_{k}(0)-\tilde{Q}_{\sigma(k)}(0)|\leq(\log N)^{5};\qquad|Q_{k}(t)-\tilde{Q}_{\sigma(k)}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{5},\end{aligned}

and

(8.24)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|&\displaystyle\sum_{i=\tilde{N}_{1}}^{\tilde{N}_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}-\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\sigma(k))})\cdot\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|\\
&\qquad-\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq(\log N)^{9}.\end{aligned}

Taking
i=k
in the first statement of (
8.22
) (using (
2.13
) to verify (
8.21
)) yields the first bound in (
8.23
). The proofs of the second and third are entirely analogous to each other, so we only verify the third. To do so, observe by (
2.1
), (
2.3
), and (
8.19
) that, for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
,

(8.25)

\displaystyle|q_{i}(t)-\tilde{q}_{i}(t)|\leq\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|b_{i}(s)-\tilde{b}_{i}(s)|ds\leq 2T\cdot e^{-(\log N)^{3}/10}\leq 1,

as
q_{i}(0)=\tilde{q}_{i}(0)
. Additionally, (
7.3
) implies for
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
that

(8.26)

\displaystyle|q_{i}(t)-q_{j}(t)|\leq\alpha\cdot|i-j|+|i-j|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2},

by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
. Combining this with (
8.25
), (
8.22
), and the definition (
2.8
) of
Q_{k}
yields

\displaystyle|Q_{k}(t)-\tilde{Q}_{\sigma(k)}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq|q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(t)-q_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}(t)|+|\tilde{q}_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(t)-\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|\varphi_{t}(k)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))|+|\varphi_{t}(k)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2}+1

\displaystyle\leq(\log N)^{5},

which confirms the third bound in (
8.23
).

It therefore remains to verify (
8.24
). To that end, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\big\{i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:\varphi_{0}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}+2T(\log N)^{6},N_{2}-2T(\log N)^{6}\rrbracket\big\};

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\big\{i\in\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket:\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(i)\in\llbracket N_{1}+2T(\log N)^{6},N_{2}-2T(\log N)^{6}\rrbracket\big\},

and
\sigma(\mathcal{S})=\{\sigma(i):i\in\mathcal{S}\}
. First suppose that
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\setminus\mathcal{S}
. Then, since (
5.1
) (and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
) implies that
|\varphi_{t}(i)-\varphi_{0}(i)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}
, we have from (
2.13
) that
\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}=\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}
. Hence, we may restrict the second sum in (
8.24
) to
i\in\mathcal{S}
. Similarly, we may restrict the first sum in (
8.24
) to
i\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
. Therefore, we have

(8.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=\tilde{N}_{1}}^{\tilde{N}_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}-\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(i)<\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\sigma(k))})\cdot\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|\\
&\qquad\qquad-\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq A+B+C.\end{aligned}

where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{S}}\big|\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}|-\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\big|;

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{S}}\big(\big|\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}|\big|+\big|\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\big|\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big|\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(i))<\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(\sigma(i))<\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\sigma(k))}\big)-\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\big)\big|;

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\#\big((\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\cup\sigma(\mathcal{S}))\setminus(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\cap\sigma(\mathcal{S}))\big)\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i\neq\sigma(k)}\big|\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|\big|.

By our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, we have

(8.28)

\displaystyle e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\leq|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|\leq 2\log N,\quad\text{and}\quad e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\leq|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}-\tilde{\lambda}_{j}|\leq 2\log N,\quad\text{whenever $i\neq j$}.

Together with the first bound in (
8.22
), this implies that
|\log|\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(k)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\sigma(i)}|-\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}||\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
for each
i\in\mathcal{S}
, and so

(8.29)

\displaystyle A\leq Ne^{-(\log N)^{2}}\leq 1.

By the second and third bounds in (
8.22
), that are at most
500(\log N)^{3}
indices
i\in\mathcal{S}
for which

\displaystyle\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(i))<\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\sigma(k))}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(\sigma(i))<\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\sigma(k))}\big)-\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\big)\neq 0.

Since this quantity is always bounded above by
2
, (
8.28
) implies that

(8.30)

\displaystyle B\leq 2000(\log N)^{5}.

By the last inequality in (
8.22
) and the injectivity of
\sigma
, it is quickly verified that
\#((\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\cup\sigma(\mathcal{S}))\setminus(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\cap\sigma(\mathcal{S})))\leq 500(\log N)^{3}
, from which we deduce again by (
8.28
) that
C\leq 500(\log N)^{5}
. This, with (
8.27
), (
8.29
), and (
8.30
), yields (
8.24
) and thus the theorem. ∎

Next, establish
Theorem

8.1
, which proves the bound in
Theorem

8.5
uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
(Outline) .

This theorem will follow from applying
Theorem

8.5
on a mesh of times
\mathcal{T}\subset[0,T]
, and using continuity bounds to show that it continues to hold on all of
[0,T]
. Since the proof is similar to the discussions at the end of the proofs of
Theorem

2.11
and
Theorem

8.5
, we only outline it.

Let
\mathfrak{c}
denote the constant
c
from
Theorem

8.5
and, for any
t\in[0,T]
, let
\mathsf{E}(t)
denote the event on which (
8.15
) holds for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
2.13
). Denoting
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{c}/2
, let
\mathcal{T}\subset[0,T]
denote a
e^{-\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}
-mesh of
[0,T]
; by a union bound, we may restrict to the event
\mathsf{F}_{1}=\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\mathsf{E}(s)
. We further restrict to the event
\mathsf{F}_{2}
on which
Proposition

7.1
holds, with the
\mathfrak{d}
there equal to
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
here; we additionally restrict to the intersection
\mathsf{F}_{3}
of all of the events from the proof of
Theorem

8.5
.

Now fix
t\in[0,T]
, and let
s\in\mathcal{S}
be such that
|s-t|\leq e^{-\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}
. By our restriction to
\mathsf{F}_{1}
, we have from (
8.15
) that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\lambda_{k}s-Q_{k}(s)+Q_{k}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|+2\displaystyle\sum_{i:\varphi_{0}(i)<\varphi_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq(\log N)^{12}.

Since
|\lambda_{k}|\cdot|s-t|\leq\log N\cdot e^{-\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}(\log N)^{2}}\leq 1
and
|\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}||\leq(\log N)^{2}
for
i\neq k
(both by our restriction to
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(0)}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
, from the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
in the proof of
Theorem

8.5
), it suffices to show that

(8.31)

\displaystyle|Q_{k}(t)-Q_{k}(s)|\leq(\log N)^{10};\qquad\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{t}(i)<\varphi_{t}(k)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi_{s}(i)<\varphi_{s}(k)}|\leq(\log N)^{8}.

The first bound in (
8.31
) follows from the fact that

\displaystyle|Q_{k}(t)-Q_{k}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq|q_{\varphi_{t}(k)}(t)-q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(t)|+|q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(t)-q_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{s}(k)|+|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{s}(k)|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2}+|s-t|\cdot\displaystyle\max_{r\in[s,t]}|b_{\varphi_{s}(k)}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq(\log N)^{5}.

where the first statement follows from the definition (
2.8
) of
Q_{k}
; the second follows from (
8.26
), (
2.1
), and (
2.3
); and the third follows from the fact that
|\varphi_{t}(k)-\varphi_{s}(k)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
(by
Proposition

7.1
, as we restricted to
\mathsf{F}_{2}
),
Definition

2.1
, and our restriction to
\mathsf{F}_{3}\subseteq\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)
. The proof of the second bound in (
8.31
) is entirely analogous to that of (
8.2
) (using the fact that
|\varphi_{t}(j)-\varphi_{s}(j)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
for
\varphi_{s}(j)\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
, by
Proposition

7.1
and our restriction to
\mathsf{F}_{2}
). This establishes the theorem.
∎

Appendix A
Proofs of Results From
Section

3

A.1.
Proofs of
Lemma

3.11
and
Lemma

3.12

Proof of
Lemma

3.11
.

Observe that the random variable
\mathfrak{r}\in\mathbb{R}
has density
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{r}\in(r,r+dr)]=\beta^{\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}\cdot e^{-\theta r-e^{-\beta e^{-r}}}dr
. Thus, denoting

(A.1)

\displaystyle F(\theta)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\theta r-\beta e^{-r}}dr,

we have

(A.2)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\theta)=-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}re^{-\theta r-\beta e^{-r}}dr=-F(\theta)\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{r}].

Changing variables
u=\beta e^{-r}
in (
A.1
) yields

\displaystyle F(\theta)=\beta^{-\theta}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{\theta-1}e^{-u}du=\beta^{-\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta),

so the lemma follows from (
A.2
), together with the fact (recall (
2.6
)) that
\alpha=-F^{\prime}(\theta)\cdot F(\theta)^{-1}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.12
.

We may assume that
i<j
; in what follows, we abbreviate
q_{k}=q_{k}(0)
and
a_{k}=a_{k}(0)
for each
k
. By (
2.5
), we have
q_{m+1}-q_{m}=-2\log a_{m}
for any
m\in\llbracket i+1,j\rrbracket
. Since
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
is sampled from the measure
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N-1,N}
from
Definition

2.4
, all such
a_{m}
are mutually independent with the same law
\mathfrak{a}
of density
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{a}\in(a,a+da)]=2\beta^{\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}\cdot a^{2\theta-1}e^{-\beta a^{2}}da
. Hence, denoting
\mathfrak{r}=-2\log\mathfrak{a}
, each
q_{m+1}-q_{m}
has law
\mathfrak{r}
. By
Lemma

3.11
, we have
\alpha=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{r}]
, so we find for any
u\in(0,1/2)
that

(A.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[q_{j}-q_{i}\geq\alpha(j-i)+R\big]\leq e^{-uR}\cdot\mathbb{E}[e^{u(q_{j}-q_{i})}\cdot e^{\alpha u(i-j)}]=\big(e^{-uR/(j-i)}\cdot\mathbb{E}[e^{u(\mathfrak{r}-\alpha)}]\big)^{j-i},

where the first statement follows from a Markov bound, and the second from the mutual independence of the
(q_{m+1}-q_{m})
with the finiteness of the moment generating function
\mathbb{E}[e^{u\mathfrak{r}}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{a}^{-2u}]<\infty
for
u\in(0,1/2)
. Setting
u=\min\{cR/(j-i),c\}
for some sufficiently small constant
c>0
, and using the (quickly verified) fact that
e^{-uR/(j-i)}\cdot\mathbb{E}[e^{u(\mathfrak{r}-\alpha)}]\leq e^{-cu^{2}}
for this
u
, yields from (
A.3
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[q_{j}-q_{i}\geq\alpha(j-i)+R\big]\leq e^{-cu^{2}|i-j|}\leq e^{-c^{3}R^{2}/|j-i|}+e^{-c^{3}|i-j|}.

By similar reasoning, we have
\mathbb{P}[q_{j}-q_{i}\leq\alpha(j-i)-R]\leq e^{-c^{3}R^{2}/|i-j|}+e^{-c^{3}|i-j|}
, and so the lemma follows by a union bound.
∎

A.2.
Proofs of Results From
Section

3.2
and
Section

3.3

Proof of
Lemma

3.5
.

Let
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
denote the eigenvalue of
\bm{L}(t)
with maximal absolute value; if more than one exists, we select one arbitrarily. Then,

(A.4)

\displaystyle|\lambda|\leq\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathscr{I}}\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathscr{I}}|L_{ij}(t^{\prime})|\leq 2\big(A(t^{\prime})+B(t^{\prime})\big).

where the first inequality holds since
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(t^{\prime})
(by
Lemma

2.2
), and the second inequality holds by
Definition

2.1
. Moreover,

(A.5)

\displaystyle|\lambda|\geq\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathscr{I}}|L_{ii}(t)|=B(t);\qquad|\lambda|\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\mathscr{I}}\big|L_{ii}(t)+2L_{ij}(t)+L_{jj}(t)\big|\geq A(t)-B(t),

where, in both statements, the first inequality follows from applying the min-max principle (to
\bm{L}(t)
if
|\lambda|\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
, and to
-\bm{L}(t)
if
-|\lambda|\in\eig\bm{L}(t)
), and the second inequality follows from
Definition

2.1
. Combining (
A.4
) and (
A.5
) yields
A(t)+B(t)\leq 3|\lambda|\leq 6(A(t^{\prime})+B(t^{\prime}))
, confirming the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.7
.

Set
\eig\bm{A}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
and
\eig\bm{B}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n})
. Further let
(\bm{u}_{j})
and
(\bm{v}_{j})
, for
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, denote orthonormal eigenbases for
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
, respectively. In this way,
\bm{u}_{j}=(u_{j}(i))_{i\in\mathscr{I}}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
, and
\bm{v}_{j}=(v_{j}(i))_{i\in\mathscr{I}}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{B}
with eigenvalue
\mu_{j}
, for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Let
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
be such that
\lambda_{k}=\lambda
; we may assume that the eigenbasis
(\bm{u}_{j})
includes
\bm{u}
, in particular, that
\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{u}
. Then,

(A.6)

\displaystyle\Imaginary G_{\varphi\varphi}(z)=\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}(\varphi)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{j}-z)^{-1}\geq u_{k}(\varphi)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda-z)^{-1}\geq\eta^{-1}\zeta^{2}.

where in the first statement we used (
3.2
); in the second we used the bound
\Imaginary(\lambda_{j}-z)^{-1}\geq 0
; and in the third we used the fact that
z=\lambda+\mathrm{i}\eta
and the second estimate in (
3.5
). It follows that

(A.7)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\displaystyle\frac{v_{j}(\varphi)^{2}}{\mu_{j}-z}\Bigg|=|H_{\varphi\varphi}(z)|\geq\big|G_{\varphi\varphi}(z)\big|-\delta\geq\eta^{-1}\zeta^{2}-\delta\geq(2\eta)^{-1}\zeta^{2},

where the first statement follows from (
3.2
); the second from the third bound in (
3.5
); the third from (
A.6
); and the fourth from the first bound in (
3.5
).

Therefore, there exists
m\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
such that
|\mu_{m}-\lambda|\leq 3n\zeta^{-2}\eta
and
|v_{m}(\varphi)|\geq(6n)^{-1/2}\zeta
. Indeed, assuming otherwise, we would have

\displaystyle(2\eta)^{-1}\zeta^{2}\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\displaystyle\frac{v_{j}(\varphi)^{2}}{\mu_{j}-z}\Bigg|<n\cdot(3n\zeta^{-2}\eta)^{-1}+n\cdot\eta^{-1}\cdot\big((6n)^{-1/2}\zeta\big)^{2}=(2\eta)^{-1}\zeta^{2},

where the first bound follows from (
A.7
) and the second from our assumption (with the fact that
z=\lambda+\mathrm{i}\eta
). This is a contradiction, which establishes the lemma.
∎

A.3.
Proofs of Results From
Section

3.4

Proof of
Lemma

3.8
.

For any
E\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\eig\bm{M}
, denote the resolvent
\bm{G}(E)=[G_{ij}(E)]=(\bm{M}-E)^{-1}
. By (
3.2
) (with the fact by
[
15
, Proposition 2.40(a)]
that all eigenvalues of
\bm{M}
are mutually disjoint), we have

(A.8)

\displaystyle u_{N_{1}}\cdot u_{N_{2}}=\displaystyle\lim_{E\rightarrow\mu}(\mu-E)\cdot G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E).

To evaluate the right side of (
A.8
), we let
\bm{C}(E)=[C_{ij}(E)]
denote the cofactor matrix of
\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id
and use the fact that
G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)\cdot(\det\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id)^{-1}
. Since removing the row of index
N_{1}
and column of index
N_{2}
from
\bm{M}
yields an upper triangular
(N-1)\times(N-1)
matrix with diagonal entries
(M_{i,i+1})_{N_{1}\leq i<N_{2}}
, we deduce that
C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)=\prod_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}M_{i,i+1}
. Hence,

(A.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)&=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)\cdot(\det\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id)^{-1}\\
&=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}M_{i,i+1}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{\mu^{\prime}\in\eig\bm{M}}(\mu^{\prime}-E)^{-1}.\end{aligned}

Combining (
A.9
) with (
A.8
) yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.9
.

Since
\bm{M}\cdot\bm{u}=\mu\cdot\bm{u}
and
\bm{M}
is tridiagonal, we have for any index
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
that
M_{k,k+1}\cdot u_{k+1}=(\mu-M_{k,k})\cdot u_{k}-M_{k,k-1}\cdot u_{k-1}
. Since
M_{k,k-1}=M_{k-1,k}
, this is equivalent to
\bm{S}_{k}(\mu)\cdot\bm{w}_{k}=\bm{w}_{k+1}
, so the lemma follows by induction on
j-i
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.10
.

It quickly follows from the explicit forms (
3.4
) of
\bm{S}_{k}
and (
3.8
) of
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{K}}
that there exist monic polynomials
P_{\ell-1}
,
P_{\ell}
,
Q_{\ell-2}
, and
Q_{\ell-1}
satisfying the following two properties. First, we have

\displaystyle\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(E)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-Q_{\ell-2}(E)\cdot M_{i-1,i}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{k=i}^{j-1}M_{k,k+1}^{-1}&P_{\ell-1}(E)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{k=i}^{j-1}M_{k,k+1}^{-1}\\
-Q_{\ell-1}(E)\cdot M_{i-1,i}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{k=i}^{j}M_{k,k+1}^{-1}&P_{\ell}(E)\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{k=i}^{j}M_{k,k+1}^{-1}\end{array}\right].

Second, we have that
\deg Q_{\ell-2}=\ell-2
; that
\deg P_{\ell-1}=\deg Q_{\ell-1}=\ell-1
; and that
\deg P_{\ell}=\ell
. Thus, it suffices to show that

(A.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &P_{\ell}(E)=\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\big(E-\mu_{h}^{[i,j]}\big);\qquad\qquad P_{\ell-1}(E)=\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{\ell-1}\big(E-\mu_{h}^{[i,j-1]}\big);\\
&Q_{\ell-1}(E)=\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{\ell-1}\big(E-\mu_{h}^{[i+1,j]}\big);\qquad Q_{\ell-2}(E)=\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{\ell-2}\big(E-\mu_{h}^{[i+1,j-1]}\big).\end{aligned}

To that end, we locate the zeroes of
P_{\ell}
. Fix any
\mu\in\eig\bm{M}^{[i,j]}
, and let
\bm{v}=(v_{i},v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
denote an eigenvector of
\bm{M}^{[i,j]}
with eigenvalue
\mu
. Then setting
\bm{w}_{k}=(v_{k-1},v_{k})
for each
k\in\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
, with
v_{i-1}=0
, we have from
Lemma

3.9
that
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j-1\rrbracket}(\mu)\cdot\bm{w}_{i}=\bm{w}_{j}
. Furthermore, the second coordinate of
S_{j}(\mu)\cdot\bm{w}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is equal

\displaystyle M_{j,j+1}^{-1}\cdot\big((\mu-M_{j,j})v_{j}-M_{j-1,j}v_{j-1}\big)=M_{j,j+1}^{-1}\cdot\big(\mu v_{j}-M_{j,j}v_{j}-M_{j,j-1}v_{j-1}\big)=0,

where the first statement follows from the fact that
\bm{M}
is symmetric and the second from the fact that
\bm{v}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{M}^{[i,j]}
with eigenvalue
\mu
. Therefore, the second coordinate of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(\mu)\cdot\bm{w}_{i}
is equal to
0
. Since the first coordinate of
\bm{w}_{i}
is equal to
v_{i-1}=0
(and its second coordinate is nonzero), this implies that the
(2,2)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(\mu)
is equal to
0
. Hence,
P_{\ell}(\mu)=0
for each
\mu\in\eig\bm{M}^{[i,j]}
. Since
\deg P_{\ell}=\ell
and
P_{\ell}
is monic, this confirms the first statement in (
A.10
).

We can now quickly deduce the remaining equalities in (
A.10
). Indeed, the second statement in (
A.10
) follows from the first, together with the fact that the
(1,2)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(E)
is equal to the
(2,2)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j-1\rrbracket}(E)
(by (
3.4
) and (
3.8
)). The third statement in (
A.10
) follows from the first, together with the fact that the
(2,1)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(E)
is equal to the
(2,2)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i+1,j\rrbracket}(E)
multiplied by
-M_{i,i+1}^{-1}M_{i-1,i}
(again by (
3.4
) and (
3.8
)). The fourth statement in (
A.10
) follows from the third, again together with the fact that the
(1,1)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}(E)
is equal to the
(2,1)
-entry of
\bm{S}_{\llbracket i,j-1\rrbracket}(E)
. This establishes the lemma.
∎

A.4.
Proofs of Results From
Section

3.5

Proof of
Lemma

3.15
.

By
Lemma

3.5
and
Lemma

2.2
, it suffices to verify the lemma at
t=0
, that is, to show
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(0)}(A)]\geq 1-c^{-1}Ne^{-cA^{2}}
. To that end, observe by
Definition

2.4
(or
Definition

3.3
) and a union bound, there is a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\max_{a\in\bm{a}(0)}|a|+\displaystyle\max_{b\in\bm{b}(0)}|b|\geq\displaystyle\frac{A}{4}\Bigg]\leq CNA^{2\theta}e^{-\beta A^{2}/2}.

By the deterministic bound (
A.4
), this yields
\mathbb{P}[\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}(0)}|\lambda|\geq A]\leq CNA^{2\theta}e^{-\beta A^{2}/2}
. Together, these two estimates imply
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(0)}(A)]\geq 1-2CNA^{2\theta}e^{-\beta A^{2}/2}
, which (as mentioned above) implies the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.18
.

Denote
\eig\bm{L}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{N})
, and define the events

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{N-1}\{\lambda_{i}\geq\lambda_{i+1}+\delta\};\qquad\mathsf{F}=\bigg\{\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\lambda_{i}|\leq 3N\bigg\}.

Let
\mathcal{J}=\llbracket-4\delta^{-1}N,4\delta^{-1}N\rrbracket
. For each
j\in\mathcal{I}
, set
z_{j}=j\delta+\delta\mathrm{i}
, and let

\displaystyle S=\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}\Imaginary(\lambda_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-z_{j})^{-1}.

Observe on
\mathsf{E}^{\complement}\cap\mathsf{F}
that there exists some
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket
such that
\Imaginary(\lambda_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}\geq(2\delta)^{-1}
and
\Imaginary(\lambda_{i+1}-z_{j})^{-1}\geq(2\delta)^{-1}
. Hence, on
\mathsf{E}^{\complement}\cap\mathsf{F}
we have
S\geq(2\delta)^{-2}
, so taking expectations yields

(A.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}\cap\mathsf{F}\big]\leq(2\delta)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[S].

Now, set
\bm{G}(z_{j})=[G_{ik}(z_{j})]=(\bm{L}-z_{j})^{-1}
for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
. By
[
44
, Equations (2.64) and (2.65)]
,
12
12

12

The results there are stated for a different random matrix, but it is quickly verified that its proofs apply for the one
\bm{L}
we study.
there exists a constant
C_{0}>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[S]=\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq N}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\det\bigg[\begin{array}[]{cc}\Imaginary G_{ii}(z_{j})&\Imaginary G_{ik}(z_{j})\\
\Imaginary G_{ik}(z_{j})&\Imaginary G_{kk}(z_{j})\end{array}\bigg]\Bigg]\leq C_{0}N^{2}|\mathcal{J}|.

Together with (
A.11
) and the fact that
|\mathcal{J}|\leq 9\delta^{-1}N
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}\cap\mathsf{F}]\leq 36C_{0}\delta N^{3}.

Since
Lemma

3.15
yields constants
C_{1}>1>c_{1}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{F}^{\complement}]\leq C_{1}e^{-c_{1}N^{2}}
, this with a union bound yields the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Heuristics for Eigenvalue Velocities

Throughout this section, we adopt
Assumption

2.8
. For each
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
t\geq 0
, define
v_{k}(t)
of
Q_{k}
by setting
Q_{k}(t)=Q_{k}(0)+t\cdot v_{k}(t)
. This quantity
v_{k}(t)
can be thought of as the “velocity” of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
under the Toda lattice. In this section, following the physics literature (for example,
[
16
]
), we explain how
Theorem

2.11
can be used to heuristically derive the large
T
limit for
v_{k}(T)
, which will coincide with the predictions of
[
16
, Equation (90)]
and
[
53
, Equation (6.19)]
. The discussion in this section is not entirely rigorous, and we do not know of a direct way to mathematically justify it completely; the sequel work
[
1
]
will be devoted to establishing its output (when
\theta
is sufficiently small) through another route. In what follows, we let
T\gg 1
be large; abbreviate
v_{k}(T)=v_{k}
; and suppose for notational simplicity that
\alpha>0
(the case when
\alpha<0
is entirely analogous).

Applying the asymptotic scattering relation (
2.14
), we obtain with high probability that

(B.1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}\approx v_{k}+2T^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|.

Any eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
should (by
Corollary

5.6
, for example) approximately only depend on the entries of the Lax matrix
\bm{L}
with indices close to
\varphi_{0}(i)
. Since the entries of
\bm{L}
are independent under thermal equilibrium, this indicates that
\lambda_{i}
should be approximately independent from most of the other
\lambda_{j}
, and from
Q_{i}(0)
(where the latter holds since the
a
-entries in
\bm{L}
depend on only the differences
q_{i+1}-q_{i}
). Now, let us assume that
v_{k}\approx v(\lambda_{k})
asymptotically only depends on
\lambda_{k}
(that it, it is approximately independent from the other
(\lambda_{j})_{j\neq k}
).
13
13

13

We are unaware of an (even heuristic) explanation for this, and so the proofs in
[
1
]
will proceed differently (based on regularizing the indicator functions), instead.
By the above approximate independence, this suggests that
\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)}
should behave as
\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{j}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{j}(0)<Q_{k}(0)}
, for a uniformly random index
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. Therefore, the sum on the right side of (
B.1
) should approximate

(B.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\approx\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq k}\mathfrak{N}_{i}\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|,

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{i}=N^{-1}\cdot\big(\#\{j:Q_{j}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})\}-\#\{j:Q_{j}(0)<Q_{k}(0)\}\big).

To approximate
\mathfrak{N}
, recall under thermal equilibrium that the
q_{i+1}-q_{i}
are identically distributed under thermal equilibrium (due to (
2.3
), since the
a_{i}
are, by
Corollary

4.8
) and that
\mathbb{E}[q_{i+1}-q_{i}]=\alpha
(by
Lemma

3.11
). Therefore,
\mathfrak{N}_{i}\approx N^{-1}\cdot\alpha^{-1}\cdot T(v_{k}-v_{i})
, which upon insertion into (
B.2
) yields

(B.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}(&\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)}-\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)+T(v_{k}-v_{i})})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\approx T(\alpha N)^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq k}(v_{k}-v_{i})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|.\end{aligned}

Now, the
(\lambda_{j})
are eigenvalues of the random Lax matrix
\bm{L}
. The empirical spectral distribution of the latter is known (from
[
41
, Lemma 4.3]
) to converge to an explicit probability density
\varrho(x)dx
. Since
v_{i}=v(\lambda_{i})
is a function of
\lambda_{i}
, we expect the above sum to likely concentrate, namely,

(B.4)

\displaystyle(\alpha N)^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq k}(v_{k}-v_{i})\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\approx\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(v(\lambda_{k})-v(\lambda)\big)\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda.

Together, the approximations (
B.1
), (
B.3
), and (
B.4
) yield

(B.5)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}\approx v(\lambda_{k})+2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(v(\lambda_{k})-v(\lambda)\big)\cdot\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda.

Replacing the approximation in (
B.5
) with an equality yields a linear system of equations for
v
that coincides with
[
16
, Equation (90)]
and
[
53
, Equation (6.19)]
. It was explained in
[
53
, Equation (6.21)]
that this system has a specific solution
v(\lambda)=v_{\eff}(\lambda)
, given by
[
53
, Equation (6.20)]
(and
[
16
, Equation (93)]
); presumably, this solution
v_{\eff}
is unique. This would then indicate
v_{k}\approx v_{\eff}(\lambda_{k})
.
